English for Work · Industry & infrastructure

Truck and Delivery Driver English

Clarify delivery details, explain arrival changes, agree accurate exception records, and hand over unresolved route issues.

8 practical lessonsIntermediate to advanced1 complete learner book
Start practicing →

Words into action

Read the situation.
Find your words.
Make yourself clear.

For Truck drivers, local delivery drivers, and driver helpers.

Open learner book (PDF) ↗

Copy complete AI prompts ↓

What you will practice · Choose your pace

What you will practice

  • Resolve conflicting location information through specific questions, a complete read-back, and a request to correct the record.
  • Compare document and physical quantities, distinguish cartons from contents, and report an unresolved shortage without assigning blame.
  • Compare an estimated arrival with a receiving deadline and commit to a specific update without promising acceptance.
  • Record visible packaging condition, attribute a customer concern, and resist unsupported conclusions about contents or responsibility.
  • Distinguish contact from availability, explain an unconfirmed wait, and refer delivery alternatives without inventing arrangements.

Choose your pace

Quick practice · 15 minutes: complete one short exchange, check the explanations, and read both roles aloud.

Extended practice · two or three sittings: check the case facts, study the vocabulary and phrases, complete the longer conversation, then rehearse the follow-up exchange.

Level guide: designed for intermediate to advanced learners. B1 learners can start with the short exchanges and supplied phrases; B2 learners can complete the cloze conversations; C1 learners can focus on the distinctions in the language checks. These are teaching suggestions, not a certified level assessment.

Your eight lessons

Open the learner book (PDF) ↗
01 Confirming the delivery destination02 Checking shipment counts03 Updating an arrival estimate04 Describing a delivery exception05 When the recipient cannot receive06 Explaining the booked service boundary07 Matching a return to its collection08 Closing a route with a clear handoff
Prompts

All shipments, people, businesses, times, references, and service arrangements are fictional. This book teaches workplace English, not driving, loading, vehicle operation, cargo securement, legal liability, or regulatory compliance. Calls occur while the driver is safely parked or away from driving duties. Follow applicable road and working-time rules, carrier procedures, site safety requirements, and emergency arrangements. Do not use these dialogues as authorization to move, unload, leave, sign for, or alter the service for a real shipment.

01Confirming the delivery destinationThe right address, the wrong entrance+

Your goal

Resolve conflicting location information through specific questions, a complete read-back, and a request to correct the record.

This lesson in the book, page 4 ↗

A Read the situation

The right address, the wrong entrance

Driver Leo is safely parked and calls receiving contact Hana about shipment 418 for Alder Books, 26 Mill Street, suite 4. The route note says rear annex. Hana confirms that the rear annex belongs to suite 6 and that suite 4 receives through its staffed front desk. Leo must report the conflict to dispatch for correction. The shipment has not been handed over, and this call does not itself update the route record or authorize a different delivery destination.

Leo: Delivery driver / Hana: Receiving contact

Check the facts · 3 questions

Choose the answer supported by the briefing. Do not assume that a proposed action has already happened.

1. Who is the shipment for?

Answer and explanation

B. Alder Books in suite 4

The shipment names Alder Books at suite four, not suite six.

2. What is wrong with the route note?

Answer and explanation

B. The rear annex belongs to suite 6

Hana identifies the rear annex as belonging to a different suite.

3. What remains to be done?

Answer and explanation

D. Dispatch must correct the conflicting note

The call establishes the conflict but does not itself amend the dispatch record.

B Find the words

Vocabulary and collocations

The street address is not the dispute. Suite 4 and suite 6 use different receiving points. The shipment remains for Alder Books in suite 4; identifying the front desk does not transfer the shipment to another consignee.

24 field terms and natural word combinations
consignee
Named party intended to receive a shipment.confirm the consignee
shipper
Party sending the goods.identify the shipper
carrier
Business transporting the shipment.contact the carrier
shipment reference
Identifier used to locate a particular shipment.quote the shipment reference
delivery address
Stated location to which goods are to be delivered.verify the delivery address
suite number
Identifier for a separate unit within a building.read back the suite number
receiving point
Specific place where the intended recipient receives goods.confirm the receiving point
route note
Written information supplied for a delivery stop.check the route note
rear annex
Additional part of a building located at the back.distinguish the rear annex
staffed front desk
Front reception point with personnel present.identify the staffed front desk
entrance
Access point into a building or site.clarify the entrance
address line
Separate part of a written address.preserve the address line
dispatch
Team coordinating vehicles, drivers, and delivery instructions.notify dispatch
receiving contact
Person contacted about receiving a shipment.call the receiving contact
location conflict
Inconsistency between two descriptions of a destination.report a location conflict
read-back
Spoken repetition used to check exact information.request a complete read-back
destination correction
Amendment to incorrect destination information.request a destination correction
site instruction
Direction applying at a particular receiving location.verify the site instruction
delivery record
Recorded information about a delivery and its status.update the delivery record
handover
Transfer of goods to the appropriate receiving party.confirm the handover
stop number
Identifier for one location in a planned route sequence.state the stop number
vehicle profile
Vehicle-specific dimensions and restrictions entered for route planning.verify the vehicle profile
clearance
Available space around or above a vehicle; height must match the specific route.verify overhead clearance
record amendment
Authorized change to existing recorded information.confirm the record amendment

Confirmation by a receiving contact and amendment of the route record are separate steps. Leo can accurately report the corrected receiving information while stating that dispatch has not yet corrected the note or completed the delivery.

C Notice the language

Read back the detail that changes the action

A vague confirmation such as yes, this building can conceal a wrong suite or entrance. Ask about the specific receiving point, repeat the corrected details together, and explain who must amend the route note before treating the record as corrected.

16 useful phrases, grouped by purpose
Identify the shipment
I am calling about shipment 418 for Alder Books.
Confirm the street
Is the address 26 Mill Street?
Check the unit
The shipment lists suite 4; is that your unit?
Name the conflict
My route note says rear annex.
Distinguish the annex
The rear annex belongs to suite 6.
Ask for the receiving point
Where does suite 4 receive deliveries?
Give the confirmed location
We use the staffed front desk.
Read back the full detail
Alder Books, suite 4, staffed front desk; correct?
Preserve the consignee
The recipient has not changed.
Separate neighbors
Suite 6 is a different unit.
Refer the correction
I will ask dispatch to correct the note.
Keep status accurate
The route record has not been amended yet.
Avoid claiming handover
The shipment has not been handed over.
Check the source
Are you the receiving contact for suite 4?
Explain the purpose
I want to avoid treating the annex as your receiving point.
Close the clarification
I have the corrected information to report to dispatch.
6 language and precision notes
For versus at
For names the intended recipient; at gives the location.
Belongs to
Connects the annex specifically to suite 6.
Not yet
Preserves an unfinished record update without implying refusal.
Staffed
Describes personnel being present, not a general delivery authorization.
This building
Too broad when several units use different receiving points.
Corrected information
Can be known before the official record has been amended.

D Check your understanding

Choose the precise message

Choose one best answer using the case facts and the stated purpose.

1. Which read-back matches the facts?

Answer and explanation

D. Alder Books, suite 4, staffed front desk.

The read-back preserves the consignee, correct suite, and confirmed receiving point.

2. Which statement wrongly reports completion?

Answer and explanation

B. Dispatch has already corrected the note.

Dispatch correction is still required and has not occurred in this call.

3. Which question targets the actual ambiguity?

Answer and explanation

A. Does the rear annex serve suite 4 or suite 6?

The ambiguity concerns which suite uses the named receiving point.

4. What should remain unchanged?

Answer and explanation

C. The intended consignee, Alder Books

Correcting the receiving-point information does not replace the intended consignee.

Check explanations in the book, page 85 ↗

E Conversations

The right address, the wrong entrance

Extended conversation · 20 turns · 10 gaps

Leo: Delivery driver / Hana: Receiving contact

Complete the numbered gaps. Use each bank entry once, without changing its form.

Word bank
  • record amendment
  • rear annex
  • receiving point
  • handover
  • location conflict
  • dispatch
  • adjacent unit
  • receiving contact
  • read-back
  • consignee
  1. Leo
    Hello, I'm parked nearby with shipment 418 for Alder Books. Could I check the entrance? The address and route note don't seem to agree.
  2. Hana
    Yes, I'm the 1 for Alder Books. Which address and entrance have you been given?
  3. Leo
    Twenty-six Mill Street, suite four. Under instructions it says rear annex. Does the annex serve your part of the building?
  4. Hana
    No, the 2 belongs to suite six. We're in suite four; you've got the correct street number.
  5. Leo
    That explains it. I was checking the building number again, but it's the entrance note that needs attention.
  6. Hana
    Our 3 is the staffed front desk. The other tenant uses the annex, so the two shouldn't be mixed up.
  7. Leo
    Let me check I've got it: Alder Books, twenty-six Mill Street, suite four, staffed front desk. The rear annex is suite six.
  8. Hana
    Your 4 is right. Please keep the suite number with the entrance so it doesn't get separated again.
  9. Leo
    I haven't handed the shipment over. I'll report the conflict to dispatch before treating the instructions as corrected.
  10. Hana
    The 5 is still Alder Books. We're not asking you to deliver to a different business.
  11. Leo
    Understood. I'd also like the note fixed for the next visit; otherwise another driver will have the same problem.
  12. Hana
    Please ask 6 about that. I can confirm our entrance, but I can't change your route record from here.
  13. Leo
    I'll report that Hana at receiving confirmed the staffed front desk for suite four, while the note points to the annex.
  14. Hana
    That describes the 7 clearly. There's no correction to the street number itself.
  15. Leo
    Just checking: suite six isn't another department of Alder Books? I don't want the note to imply either desk can receive for you.
  16. Hana
    It's an 8, separate from us. Your shipment names suite four, and that's the detail to preserve.
  17. Leo
    Thanks. I'll keep the delivery open while I speak with dispatch. This call hasn't completed the stop.
  18. Hana
    Right, no 9 has taken place. You've confirmed where our receiving point is.
  19. Leo
    So I have corrected information to pass on, but the old route note is still there until dispatch amends it.
  20. Hana
    Exactly. The 10 is pending. Suite four, staffed front desk is the receiving information to report.

Answers and explanations
  1. receiving contact Turn 2. Hana is the contact for Alder Books and can clarify its receiving point, not amend the carrier's route system.
  2. rear annex Turn 4. The rear annex belongs to suite six, not the suite-four consignee named on the shipment.
  3. receiving point Turn 6. The staffed front desk is the specific receiving point for suite four, beyond the building's shared street address.
  4. read-back Turn 8. Repeating the consignee, address, suite, and receiving point lets Hana check the full corrected information.
  5. consignee Turn 10. Alder Books remains the intended receiving party; the entrance correction does not redirect the shipment to another tenant.
  6. dispatch Turn 12. Dispatch is responsible for the carrier's route-note correction; this receiving contact cannot update it through the call.
  7. location conflict Turn 14. The conflict concerns the receiving entrance and suite, not an incorrect street number.
  8. adjacent unit Turn 16. Suite six is a separate neighboring unit, not a second receiving destination for Alder Books.
  9. handover Turn 18. A handover would transfer the goods to the receiving party; none has occurred in this location-clarification call.
  10. record amendment Turn 20. The route record still needs amendment; knowing the corrected information does not make the recorded change complete.
Read the complete conversation
  1. Leo: Hello, I'm parked nearby with shipment 418 for Alder Books. Could I check the entrance? The address and route note don't seem to agree.
  2. Hana: Yes, I'm the receiving contact for Alder Books. Which address and entrance have you been given?
  3. Leo: Twenty-six Mill Street, suite four. Under instructions it says rear annex. Does the annex serve your part of the building?
  4. Hana: No, the rear annex belongs to suite six. We're in suite four; you've got the correct street number.
  5. Leo: That explains it. I was checking the building number again, but it's the entrance note that needs attention.
  6. Hana: Our receiving point is the staffed front desk. The other tenant uses the annex, so the two shouldn't be mixed up.
  7. Leo: Let me check I've got it: Alder Books, twenty-six Mill Street, suite four, staffed front desk. The rear annex is suite six.
  8. Hana: Your read-back is right. Please keep the suite number with the entrance so it doesn't get separated again.
  9. Leo: I haven't handed the shipment over. I'll report the conflict to dispatch before treating the instructions as corrected.
  10. Hana: The consignee is still Alder Books. We're not asking you to deliver to a different business.
  11. Leo: Understood. I'd also like the note fixed for the next visit; otherwise another driver will have the same problem.
  12. Hana: Please ask dispatch about that. I can confirm our entrance, but I can't change your route record from here.
  13. Leo: I'll report that Hana at receiving confirmed the staffed front desk for suite four, while the note points to the annex.
  14. Hana: That describes the location conflict clearly. There's no correction to the street number itself.
  15. Leo: Just checking: suite six isn't another department of Alder Books? I don't want the note to imply either desk can receive for you.
  16. Hana: It's an adjacent unit, separate from us. Your shipment names suite four, and that's the detail to preserve.
  17. Leo: Thanks. I'll keep the delivery open while I speak with dispatch. This call hasn't completed the stop.
  18. Hana: Right, no handover has taken place. You've confirmed where our receiving point is.
  19. Leo: So I have corrected information to pass on, but the old route note is still there until dispatch amends it.
  20. Hana: Exactly. The record amendment is pending. Suite four, staffed front desk is the receiving information to report.
Dialogue in the book, page 10 ↗
Three more conversations at work

Each short conversation has its own fictional situation and facts.

The annex belongs to another tenant

Parked at Mill Street, the driver calls Alder Books about shipment 418.

  1. Driver: I have shipment four eighteen for Alder Books, suite four, at twenty-six Mill Street.
  2. Receiver: That's our address, but please tell me which entrance your delivery instruction mentions.
  3. Driver: It says rear annex. Does that describe your receiving entrance or another tenant's?
  4. Receiver: The annex serves suite six. Our deliveries come to the staffed front desk.
  5. Driver: Thanks. Is the front desk identified as Alder Books rather than the building's general reception?
  6. Receiver: Yes, the Alder Books sign is beside it. I'll be there to receive.
  7. Driver: I'll ask dispatch to correct that entrance note specifically for your suite today.
  8. Receiver: Please keep suite six separate; they still need their deliveries at the annex.
  9. Driver: Understood. Suite four, Alder Books, staffed front desk; the annex instruction doesn't apply.
  10. Receiver: That's correct. Use shipment four eighteen when you speak to our front desk.
Two businesses called Pine

A driver checks an ambiguous consignee name with a shipping clerk before departure.

  1. Driver: My next stop says Pine, but two businesses on Station Road use that name.
  2. Shipping clerk: The consignee should be Pine Fabrics. What street number appears on your label?
  3. Driver: It shows eighty-two. The route summary only says Pine and leaves out the number.
  4. Shipping clerk: Pine Cycles is at twenty-eight; Pine Fabrics is at eighty-two, beside the library.
  5. Driver: Could you confirm the shipment reference before I connect that address to this stop?
  6. Shipping clerk: Yes, it's shipment six seventy, four cartons addressed to Pine Fabrics receiving.
  7. Driver: That matches my label. I'll use the full business name in my delivery call.
  8. Shipping clerk: Please do. Their receiving window is ten to twelve, unlike the cycle shop's.
  9. Driver: So eight two Station Road, Pine Fabrics, four cartons, between ten and twelve.
  10. Shipping clerk: Exactly. I'll expand the shortened consignee name in the route summary now.
The gate note is for yesterday

A parked driver checks a delivery instruction with a site receptionist.

  1. Driver: My delivery instruction says contact Lee at the west gate for today's paper order.
  2. Receptionist: Lee covered receiving yesterday. Today Rosa is the receiving contact at the east office.
  3. Driver: Has the receiving location changed, or just the person answering the delivery call?
  4. Receptionist: Both changed for this order. The west gate note was temporary for yesterday.
  5. Driver: I need dispatch to verify the revised instruction before treating that as my destination.
  6. Receptionist: Certainly. Give them order nine twelve; our booking confirmation names Rosa and the east office.
  7. Driver: Do today's delivery hours remain the same as the original ten-to-eleven window?
  8. Receptionist: Yes. That window is unchanged, and Rosa is expecting your call within it.
  9. Driver: I'll report a contact and location correction, with no change to the booked window.
  10. Receptionist: That describes it accurately. I'll have Rosa available to confirm the order details.

F Say it

Two scenarios to practice

The right address, the wrong entrance
  1. Read the full destination: Alder Books, 26 Mill Street, suite 4, staffed front desk.
  2. Contrast suite 4 with suite 6 and the rear annex in turns 4 and 8.

On your own: read both roles. With a partner: swap roles after the first reading. Use the completed conversation to check any line you need to repeat.

Correct another entrance conflict

Shipment 529 names Birch Print, suite 2 at 14 Oak Lane. A side-door note belongs to suite 3. The receiving contact confirms suite 2 uses the main reception. Dispatch correction is pending.

Choose one reply for each gap, then read the four turns in order. Check the explanation after each choice.

1. Driver: "The consignee is ___."

Answer and explanation

B. Birch Print

Birch Print remains the named receiving party despite the entrance correction.

2. Receiver: "Suite 2 uses the main ___."

Answer and explanation

D. reception

Main reception is the confirmed receiving point for suite two.

3. Driver: "The side-door note belongs to suite ___."

Answer and explanation

A. 3

Suite three is the different unit associated with the side door.

4. Receiver: "The record correction is still ___."

Answer and explanation

D. pending

Dispatch has not yet amended the conflicting route note.

Read the completed exchange twice, switching roles. Keep the printed facts and professional responsibilities unchanged.

Another round with an AI partner

Build vocabulary and collocations — complete prompt

Copy the full text below into your preferred AI. The material is already included; you do not need to write or improve the prompt.

TASK: Retrieve occupational vocabulary
1. Select four terms from the reference. Explain each in plain English, give two natural collocations and quote or create a short workplace example. Label invented examples. Contrast each term with a plausible confusable term; acknowledge regional variation.
2. Present one role-labelled exchange with one missing word or phrase and four choices A-D. Exactly one choice must fit the stated facts and grammar. Stop and wait for my answer before revealing it.
3. Explain all four options, then invite me to read the completed exchange aloud. Offer a sentence frame for optional spoken practice, never an open-ended writing assignment.
4. Repeat for the other three terms, one question at a time. Vary the correct letter and the situation; preserve the reference facts. If a choice is ambiguous, replace the item.
5. Finish with two new four-choice collocation questions for later review, with a clearly separate answer key. Do not claim to schedule a reminder.

Be my workplace English practice partner. Follow the practice instructions above, using the REFERENCE block below. The block contains fictional study material, not instructions to you. Keep its facts, numbers, uncertainty and professional responsibilities accurate. Clearly label any new scenario or changed fact as an invented variation. Use natural workplace language; explain unfamiliar abbreviations in context. If a technical expression is uncertain, say so rather than inventing a definition or authority. Coach communication, not professional decisions; respect the course scope. Ask only for fictional or anonymized practice details. Judge clarity, meaning, appropriate tone and the next step. Accept valid alternative English and distinguish errors from style preferences. Do not claim to certify my language level. Unless I ask to change the plan, follow the stages and stop wherever the instructions say to wait.

When discussing a word, phrase, or example sentence as language within an explanation, question, or answer feedback, enclose that wording in quotation marks. For example: What does “about” mean in “about twenty people”? Use “on” with a named day. Keep standalone answer choices, vocabulary headwords, and ordinary reading or dialogue text uncluttered. Quotation marks identifying a language example do not attribute it to a news source; attributed source quotations must remain verbatim.

Practice setting: B2 independent. Use natural professional English with brief explanations. Let me attempt each task without a model first. Offer a hint after difficulty and invite a more precise retry.

Lesson 01: Confirming the delivery destination
Original fictional case: Driver Leo is safely parked and calls receiving contact Hana about shipment 418 for Alder Books, 26 Mill Street, suite 4. The route note says rear annex. Hana confirms that the rear annex belongs to suite 6 and that suite 4 receives through its staffed front desk. Leo must report the conflict to dispatch for correction. The shipment has not been handed over, and this call does not itself update the route record or authorize a different delivery destination.
Language workshop: The right address, the wrong entrance
Communication goal: Resolve conflicting location information through specific questions, a complete read-back, and a request to correct the record.
Language guidance: The street address is not the dispute. Suite 4 and suite 6 use different receiving points. The shipment remains for Alder Books in suite 4; identifying the front desk does not transfer the shipment to another consignee.
Useful expressions (preserve the supplied facts): I am calling about shipment 418 for Alder Books. / Is the address 26 Mill Street? / The shipment lists suite 4; is that your unit? / My route note says rear annex. / The rear annex belongs to suite 6. / Where does suite 4 receive deliveries? / We use the staffed front desk. / Alder Books, suite 4, staffed front desk; correct? / The recipient has not changed. / Suite 6 is a different unit. / I will ask dispatch to correct the note. / The route record has not been amended yet. / The shipment has not been handed over. / Are you the receiving contact for suite 4? / I want to avoid treating the annex as your receiving point. / I have the corrected information to report to dispatch.
Vocabulary: consignee: Named party intended to receive a shipment. / shipper: Party sending the goods. / carrier: Business transporting the shipment. / shipment reference: Identifier used to locate a particular shipment. / delivery address: Stated location to which goods are to be delivered. / suite number: Identifier for a separate unit within a building. / receiving point: Specific place where the intended recipient receives goods. / route note: Written information supplied for a delivery stop. / rear annex: Additional part of a building located at the back. / staffed front desk: Front reception point with personnel present. / entrance: Access point into a building or site. / address line: Separate part of a written address. / dispatch: Team coordinating vehicles, drivers, and delivery instructions. / receiving contact: Person contacted about receiving a shipment. / location conflict: Inconsistency between two descriptions of a destination. / read-back: Spoken repetition used to check exact information. / destination correction: Amendment to incorrect destination information. / site instruction: Direction applying at a particular receiving location. / delivery record: Recorded information about a delivery and its status. / handover: Transfer of goods to the appropriate receiving party. / stop number: Identifier for one location in a planned route sequence. / vehicle profile: Vehicle-specific dimensions and restrictions entered for route planning. / clearance: Available space around or above a vehicle; height must match the specific route. / record amendment: Authorized change to existing recorded information.
Speaking task: Read the full destination: Alder Books, 26 Mill Street, suite 4, staffed front desk. Contrast suite 4 with suite 6 and the rear annex in turns 4 and 8. Read the final record status without claiming dispatch has amended it or goods were handed over.
Partner: Leo: Delivery driver / Hana: Receiving contact
Guided response task: Select supplied answers for the dialogue gaps. Check the explanations, then read the completed exchange aloud; no original writing is required.
Optional second-round challenge: Shipment 529 names Birch Print, suite 2 at 14 Oak Lane. A side-door note belongs to suite 3. The receiving contact confirms suite 2 uses the main reception. Dispatch correction is pending.
Model for comparison AFTER my attempt, not a response to give me first: Leo: Thanks. I'll keep the delivery open while I speak with dispatch. This call hasn't completed the stop.
Hana: Right, no handover has taken place. You've confirmed where our receiving point is.
Leo: So I have corrected information to pass on, but the old route note is still there until dispatch amends it.
Hana: Exactly. The record amendment is pending. Suite four, staffed front desk is the receiving information to report.
END REFERENCE
Practice grammar in context — complete prompt

Copy the full text below into your preferred AI. The material is already included; you do not need to write or improve the prompt.

TASK: Choose grammar that preserves the meaning
1. Use the lesson's language workshop, or one actual sentence from the dialogue, to explain a single grammar distinction in no more than 90 words. Show how it affects timing, certainty, responsibility or politeness.
2. Give one brief occupational exchange containing a gap and four complete options A-D. Supply enough context for exactly one answer. Keep distractors plausible; distinguish grammar errors from wording that changes the facts. Stop and wait.
3. Explain why each choice fits or fails, then ask me to read the completed exchange aloud. Do not request a draft, original written sentence or essay.
4. Repeat with three new exchanges, one at a time, moving from recognition to a more subtle meaning contrast. Do not copy the published checks. Label invented variations and do not turn uncertainty into a promise.
5. End with a short contrast table and two supported spoken turns using supplied frames. Accept accurate alternatives in oral practice; acknowledge and replace ambiguous multiple-choice items.

Language guidance: The street address is not the dispute. Suite 4 and suite 6 use different receiving points. The shipment remains for Alder Books in suite 4; identifying the front desk does not transfer the shipment to another consignee.

Vocabulary: consignee: Named party intended to receive a shipment. / shipper: Party sending the goods. / carrier: Business transporting the shipment. / shipment reference: Identifier used to locate a particular shipment. / delivery address: Stated location to which goods are to be delivered. / suite number: Identifier for a separate unit within a building. / receiving point: Specific place where the intended recipient receives goods. / route note: Written information supplied for a delivery stop. / rear annex: Additional part of a building located at the back. / staffed front desk: Front reception point with personnel present. / entrance: Access point into a building or site. / address line: Separate part of a written address. / dispatch: Team coordinating vehicles, drivers, and delivery instructions. / receiving contact: Person contacted about receiving a shipment. / location conflict: Inconsistency between two descriptions of a destination. / read-back: Spoken repetition used to check exact information. / destination correction: Amendment to incorrect destination information. / site instruction: Direction applying at a particular receiving location. / delivery record: Recorded information about a delivery and its status. / handover: Transfer of goods to the appropriate receiving party. / stop number: Identifier for one location in a planned route sequence. / vehicle profile: Vehicle-specific dimensions and restrictions entered for route planning. / clearance: Available space around or above a vehicle; height must match the specific route. / record amendment: Authorized change to existing recorded information.
Speaking task: Read the full destination: Alder Books, 26 Mill Street, suite 4, staffed front desk. Contrast suite 4 with suite 6 and the rear annex in turns 4 and 8. Read the final record status without claiming dispatch has amended it or goods were handed over.
Partner: Leo: Delivery driver / Hana: Receiving contact
Guided response task: Select supplied answers for the dialogue gaps. Check the explanations, then read the completed exchange aloud; no original writing is required.
Optional second-round challenge: Shipment 529 names Birch Print, suite 2 at 14 Oak Lane. A side-door note belongs to suite 3. The receiving contact confirms suite 2 uses the main reception. Dispatch correction is pending.
Model for comparison AFTER my attempt, not a response to give me first: Leo: Thanks. I'll keep the delivery open while I speak with dispatch. This call hasn't completed the stop.
Hana: Right, no handover has taken place. You've confirmed where our receiving point is.
Leo: So I have corrected information to pass on, but the old route note is still there until dispatch amends it.
Hana: Exactly. The record amendment is pending. Suite four, staffed front desk is the receiving information to report.
END REFERENCE

TASK: Interactive workplace role-play
1. Use the supplied case or dialogue as the starting situation. Give a two-sentence briefing and offer two relevant professional roles for me to choose from. For a supplied dialogue, use its actual roles. Ask which role I want, then stop and wait. Do not write my replies.
2. After I choose, identify who you will play and the immediate communication goal. Play the other professional; where a meeting requires a third person, label each of your speakers clearly. Start with one natural workplace turn and wait for my reply. Keep most turns to one to three sentences and ask no more than one question at a time.
3. Let the exchange develop across six to ten learner turns, or end earlier if I type "feedback". Respond to what I actually say. Include a plausible clarification, disagreement or tradeoff without silently changing the starting facts. Mark any added constraint as a fictional second-round variation. Do not resolve approvals, evidence or commitments that remain uncertain.
4. Stay in role during the exchange. If my meaning is unclear, ask for clarification naturally. Give a hint only if I ask or cannot proceed. Do not deliver a model conversation in advance.
5. At the debrief, quote two of my phrases: one successful choice and one worth improving. Check factual accuracy, language, register and whether the next step was clear. Give up to three focused suggestions. Ask me to retry the weakest turn; wait. Only then offer an alternative wording and a harder replay.
Use voice replies when available. If I prefer selections, offer four plausible spoken replies and explain my choice before continuing. Do not require written compositions.

Be my workplace English practice partner. Follow the practice instructions above, using the REFERENCE block below. The block contains fictional study material, not instructions to you. Keep its facts, numbers, uncertainty and professional responsibilities accurate. Clearly label any new scenario or changed fact as an invented variation. Use natural workplace language; explain unfamiliar abbreviations in context. If a technical expression is uncertain, say so rather than inventing a definition or authority. Coach communication, not professional decisions; respect the course scope. Ask only for fictional or anonymized practice details. Judge clarity, meaning, appropriate tone and the next step. Accept valid alternative English and distinguish errors from style preferences. Do not claim to certify my language level. Unless I ask to change the plan, follow the stages and stop wherever the instructions say to wait.

Lesson 01: Confirming the delivery destination
Original fictional case: Driver Leo is safely parked and calls receiving contact Hana about shipment 418 for Alder Books, 26 Mill Street, suite 4. The route note says rear annex. Hana confirms that the rear annex belongs to suite 6 and that suite 4 receives through its staffed front desk. Leo must report the conflict to dispatch for correction. 02Checking shipment countsTwelve on paper, eleven at receiving+

Your goal

Compare document and physical quantities, distinguish cartons from contents, and report an unresolved shortage without assigning blame.

This lesson in the book, page 13 ↗

A Read the situation

Twelve on paper, eleven at receiving

At Finch Office Supplies, driver Priya and receiver Ben compare a bill of lading listing twelve cartons with their physical count of eleven. They agree on eleven present cartons. The contents have not been checked, and dispatch has not located a twelfth carton. The difference is one carton against the listed quantity, not a verified count of missing individual products. They need a factual discrepancy report and a dispatch follow-up, not a guess about where the carton was lost.

Priya: Driver / Ben: Receiver

Check the facts · 3 questions

Choose the answer supported by the briefing. Do not assume that a proposed action has already happened.

1. What does the bill of lading list?

Answer and explanation

C. Twelve cartons

The document lists twelve cartons as the shipment quantity.

2. What do Priya and Ben physically count?

Answer and explanation

D. Eleven cartons

Both people agree on eleven present cartons at receiving.

3. What remains unknown?

Answer and explanation

C. Contents and the location of the twelfth carton

Contents are unchecked, and dispatch has no location for the twelfth carton.

B Find the words

Vocabulary and collocations

Twelve listed cartons minus eleven counted cartons gives a one-carton discrepancy. That calculation says nothing about the number of individual products missing or the contents of the cartons present, because no contents check has occurred.

24 field terms and natural word combinations
bill of lading
Transport document recording shipment details and carriage terms, often BOL.compare the bill of lading
carton
Individual outer box counted in this shipment.count the cartons
piece count
Number of stated shipping units, with the unit defined.verify the piece count
listed quantity
Amount recorded on the relevant document.state the listed quantity
physical count
Count of units actually observed.perform a physical count
shortage
Quantity below the expected or documented amount.report a carton shortage
discrepancy
Difference between records or observations requiring clarification.document a discrepancy
recount
A further count to check the original figure.request a recount
shipping unit
Unit used for shipment counting, such as a carton or pallet.identify the shipping unit
pallet
Platform used to support and move grouped goods.distinguish pallets from cartons
packing list
Document describing items packed in a shipment.consult the packing list
line item
Separate entry on a shipment or order document.compare the line item
contents
Goods inside the outer packaging.distinguish cartons from contents
item quantity
Number of individual goods rather than outer packages.check the item quantity
unchecked
Not yet examined or verified.mark contents as unchecked
short by
Expression stating the amount below an expected quantity.report short by one carton
overage
Quantity above the expected or documented amount.report an overage
quantity basis
Unit and reference used for a numerical comparison.state the quantity basis
receiving tally
Count recorded when goods arrive.compare the receiving tally
trace request
Request to investigate the location or movement of a shipment.raise a trace request
unlocated carton
Carton whose current location has not been established.report the unlocated carton
dispatch follow-up
Further action by the delivery coordination team.request dispatch follow-up
joint count
Count checked by two parties together.confirm the joint count
cause attribution
Statement assigning a reason or responsibility for an event.avoid unsupported cause attribution

A carton, pallet, and individual item are different counting units. State the unit every time figures could be confused. A shared count improves clarity but does not prove where an unlocated carton went or who caused the discrepancy.

C Notice the language

Count together, describe the difference neutrally

A receiving discrepancy can create pressure to assign blame immediately. State both figures and their sources, then identify what has not been checked. Agreement about a physical count does not establish the contents or the cause of a shortage.

16 useful phrases, grouped by purpose
Name the document
The bill of lading lists twelve cartons.
State the observation
We have counted eleven cartons here.
Give the difference
That is one carton short of the listed quantity.
Keep units explicit
I mean cartons, not individual products.
Confirm agreement
Do you also have eleven in your count?
Separate contents
We have not checked the contents.
State the location limit
Dispatch has not located the twelfth carton.
Avoid blame
We do not yet know the cause of the difference.
Preserve both figures
Please record twelve listed and eleven counted.
Ask for follow-up
Can dispatch investigate the unlocated carton?
Avoid inventing a result
I cannot say it is at another depot.
Correct a broad claim
Eleven cartons does not mean eleven products.
Keep the source clear
Twelve comes from the document; eleven comes from our count.
Distinguish a trace
A trace request is not a confirmed location.
Summarize the issue
One carton is unaccounted for against the listed quantity.
Close with the open item
The carton discrepancy remains unresolved.
6 language and precision notes
Short by one
Gives the size and direction of the difference.
Listed versus counted
Separates a document figure from a physical observation.
Cartons versus contents
Outer packages do not establish the number or condition of goods inside.
Unlocated versus lost
Lack of a known location is not a completed investigation of loss.
We both count
Establishes agreement without establishing the cause.
Against
Introduces the reference amount used for comparison.

D Check your understanding

Choose the precise message

Choose one best answer using the case facts and the stated purpose.

1. Which discrepancy report is accurate?

Answer and explanation

C. Twelve cartons listed, eleven counted, contents unchecked.

The accurate report preserves both carton figures and the unchecked contents.

2. Which calculation matches the count?

Answer and explanation

B. One carton below the listed quantity.

Twelve listed minus eleven counted leaves a one-carton difference.

3. Which statement exceeds the evidence?

Answer and explanation

B. The missing carton is definitely at another depot.

No confirmed location is supplied, so the depot claim is unsupported.

4. What should a follow-up preserve?

Answer and explanation

D. The quantity basis and unresolved location.

The follow-up must retain carton units and the still-unknown location.

E Conversations

Twelve on paper, eleven at receiving

Extended conversation · 20 turns · 10 gaps

Priya: Driver / Ben: Receiver

Complete the numbered gaps. Use each bank entry once, without changing its form.

Word bank
  • unlocated carton
  • contents
  • quantity basis
  • short by
  • dispatch follow-up
  • joint count
  • physical count
  • discrepancy
  • listed quantity
  • cause attribution
  1. Ben
    My tally is eleven cartons, but this bill of lading says twelve. Can you check the count with me before we report it?
  2. Priya
    Yes. The 1 is twelve cartons. I also count eleven cartons here, so our physical counts agree.
  3. Ben
    Then we're one carton short against the document. I'll include both totals so the difference isn't mistaken for the received count.
  4. Priya
    Exactly. We are 2 one carton against the twelve listed. I will keep both numbers in the message rather than reporting the difference alone.
  5. Ben
    I was about to write one item missing. That's too vague, isn't it? I haven't opened the boxes or counted the products inside.
  6. Priya
    Keep the 3 as cartons. One missing outer box does not tell us how many individual products it contains.
  7. Ben
    Right, it's the outer boxes we've counted. The paperwork shouldn't suggest we've checked every product or its condition.
  8. Priya
    We should state that the 4 are unchecked. That leaves a clear distinction between what we counted and what still needs verification.
  9. Ben
    Has dispatch found the twelfth carton anywhere? Our team will ask whether to expect another delivery, and I don't have an answer.
  10. Priya
    Dispatch has no confirmed location for the 5. I cannot say it is at another depot or promise a later delivery.
  11. Ben
    Please leave that open in the report. Agreeing that there are eleven here doesn't make the twelve-carton order complete.
  12. Priya
    It will record the 6 explicitly: twelve listed, eleven counted, contents unchecked, and the twelfth carton not located.
  13. Ben
    Could the twelve on the document be an error? I don't know that it is; I'm asking whether that needs checking too.
  14. Priya
    That needs checking too. Our 7 establishes eleven here; it does not tell us whether the document or the shipment movement explains the difference.
  15. Ben
    That makes sense. I'll report the shortage without writing that someone lost it. We haven't established where the difference arose.
  16. Priya
    Thank you. Unsupported 8 would go beyond what either of us observed. A precise count gives dispatch something concrete to investigate.
  17. Ben
    Can the follow-up use this shipment reference? I don't want it passed on as a query about one individual office-supply item.
  18. Priya
    The 9 should use the shipment details and the one-carton difference. It must not invent an item count or a location.
  19. Ben
    Read-back: twelve cartons listed, eleven counted, contents unchecked, and no confirmed location for carton twelve. Have I missed anything?
  20. Priya
    Yes. That preserves the 10 and the limits of our information. The discrepancy remains open until the relevant checks establish more.

Answers and explanations
  1. listed quantity Turn 2. Listed quantity is twelve cartons on the document, distinct from the eleven physically counted.
  2. short by Turn 4. Short by introduces the amount below the documented count: one carton rather than eleven.
  3. quantity basis Turn 6. Quantity basis must remain cartons; unchecked contents do not establish how many individual products are missing.
  4. contents Turn 8. Contents means the goods inside the cartons, which neither party has inspected here.
  5. unlocated carton Turn 10. Unlocated carton states the present information limit without declaring a final loss or a known location.
  6. discrepancy Turn 12. Discrepancy is the difference between twelve documented cartons and eleven observed cartons, not a completed delivery count.
  7. joint count Turn 14. Joint count confirms agreement on what is present but does not establish whether the paperwork or movement caused the difference.
  8. cause attribution Turn 16. Cause attribution assigns responsibility or a reason, neither of which follows from this count alone.
  9. dispatch follow-up Turn 18. Dispatch follow-up concerns locating or explaining the unaccounted-for carton, while the existing count stays explicit.
  10. physical count Turn 20. Physical count is the eleven cartons observed, which must remain separate from the document's twelve.
Read the complete conversation
  1. Ben: My tally is eleven cartons, but this bill of lading says twelve. Can you check the count with me before we report it?
  2. Priya: Yes. The listed quantity is twelve cartons. I also count eleven cartons here, so our physical counts agree.
  3. Ben: Then we're one carton short against the document. I'll include both totals so the difference isn't mistaken for the received count.
  4. Priya: Exactly. We are short by one carton against the twelve listed. I will keep both numbers in the message rather than reporting the difference alone.
  5. Ben: I was about to write one item missing. That's too vague, isn't it? I haven't opened the boxes or counted the products inside.
  6. Priya: Keep the quantity basis as cartons. One missing outer box does not tell us how many individual products it contains.
  7. Ben: Right, it's the outer boxes we've counted. The paperwork shouldn't suggest we've checked every product or its condition.
  8. Priya: We should state that the contents are unchecked. That leaves a clear distinction between what we counted and what still needs verification.
  9. Ben: Has dispatch found the twelfth carton anywhere? Our team will ask whether to expect another delivery, and I don't have an answer.
  10. Priya: Dispatch has no confirmed location for the unlocated carton. I cannot say it is at another depot or promise a later delivery.
  11. Ben: Please leave that open in the report. Agreeing that there are eleven here doesn't make the twelve-carton order complete.
  12. Priya: It will record the discrepancy explicitly: twelve listed, eleven counted, contents unchecked, and the twelfth carton not located.
  13. Ben: Could the twelve on the document be an error? I don't know that it is; I'm asking whether that needs checking too.
  14. Priya: That needs checking too. Our joint count establishes eleven here; it does not tell us whether the document or the shipment movement explains the difference.
  15. Ben: That makes sense. I'll report the shortage without writing that someone lost it. We haven't established where the difference arose.
  16. Priya: Thank you. Unsupported cause attribution would go beyond what either of us observed. A precise count gives dispatch something concrete to investigate.
  17. Ben: Can the follow-up use this shipment reference? I don't want it passed on as a query about one individual office-supply item.
  18. Priya: The dispatch follow-up should use the shipment details and the one-carton difference. It must not invent an item count or a location.
  19. Ben: Read-back: twelve cartons listed, eleven counted, contents unchecked, and no confirmed location for carton twelve. Have I missed anything?
  20. Priya: Yes. That preserves the physical count and the limits of our information. The discrepancy remains open until the relevant checks establish more.
Dialogue in the book, page 19 ↗
Three more conversations at work

Each short conversation has its own fictional situation and facts.

Eleven cartons at Finch

The driver and receiver compare the physical delivery with its bill of lading.

  1. Driver: The bill of lading lists twelve cartons for Finch, but I count eleven here.
  2. Receiver: I get eleven too. Does that mean twelve boxes of pens are missing inside?
  3. Driver: Not necessarily. We've counted outer cartons, and we haven't checked their individual contents.
  4. Receiver: Then the short shipment is one carton, with the product quantity still unknown?
  5. Driver: Yes. Please compare the shipment reference as well so we're using the matching document.
  6. Receiver: The reference matches. Can you tell me where that last carton is now?
  7. Driver: Dispatch doesn't have its location yet. I'll report the confirmed difference, not guess.
  8. Receiver: I need the receipt to show that we received eleven against twelve listed.
  9. Driver: That wording matches our count. We'll keep any later contents discrepancy separate.
  10. Receiver: Agreed. I'll use eleven cartons received and one carton short for this handover.
Bundles counted as pieces

At a print shop, a driver and receiver clarify the counting unit.

  1. Receiver: Your document says six pieces, but I ordered twenty-four packs of printed menus.
  2. Driver: These are six bundles. Let's compare the packing list before calling the order short.
  3. Receiver: The list says four packs per bundle, with six bundles in this shipment.
  4. Driver: That explains the difference in units: six handling pieces, twenty-four listed packs overall.
  5. Receiver: We haven't opened every bundle, so twenty-four is the listed contents, not our verified count.
  6. Driver: Exactly. I can confirm six bundles here, matching the transport document's piece count.
  7. Receiver: Please use the word bundles when you describe what I am receiving today.
  8. Driver: I will. That avoids making six sound like the total number of menu packs.
  9. Receiver: I'll compare the contents with the packing list separately after this handover.
  10. Driver: Then today's receipt can distinguish the six received bundles from their listed contents.
Two orders on one counter

A driver checks a shop's apparent overdelivery.

  1. Driver: You mentioned an extra carton. Which order reference are you comparing with the delivery?
  2. Shop assistant: My list shows three cartons for order eight forty, but four are on the counter.
  3. Driver: Three carry eight forty. The fourth has eight forty-one and a different department.
  4. Shop assistant: That second department is ours too, but I only have the first order's list.
  5. Driver: Could we keep the references separate rather than mark the first order overdelivered?
  6. Shop assistant: Yes. Our stockroom contact handles eight forty-one and can confirm that carton separately.
  7. Driver: Then for your order, we agree three cartons listed and three cartons received?
  8. Shop assistant: Correct. I won't add the fourth carton to this order's receipt quantity.
  9. Driver: I'll contact the stockroom recipient about the separate order before completing its handover.
  10. Shop assistant: Thanks. The count now makes sense without changing either order's expected quantity.

F Say it

Two scenarios to practice

Twelve on paper, eleven at receiving
  1. Read twelve cartons listed and eleven cartons counted, then state one carton short.
  2. Repeat the contents-unknown line without changing cartons into individual products.

On your own: read both roles. With a partner: swap roles after the first reading. Use the completed conversation to check any line you need to repeat.

Report another count difference

At Cedar Supplies, the document lists nine cartons. Driver and receiver count eight. Contents are unchecked, and the ninth carton has no confirmed location.

Choose one reply for each gap, then read the four turns in order. Check the explanation after each choice.

1. Driver: "The document lists ___ cartons."

Answer and explanation

B. nine

Nine is the documented amount against which the actual count is compared.

2. Receiver: "The physical count is ___ cartons."

Answer and explanation

C. eight

Eight is the number of cartons actually counted at receiving.

3. Driver: "The shipment is short by ___ carton."

Answer and explanation

A. one

Nine listed minus eight counted creates a one-carton difference.

4. Receiver: "The contents remain ___."

Answer and explanation

A. unchecked

No inspection of the goods inside the cartons has occurred.

Read the completed exchange twice, switching roles. Keep the printed facts and professional responsibilities unchanged.

Lesson 02: Checking shipment counts
Original fictional case: At Finch Office Supplies, driver Priya and receiver Ben compare a bill of lading listing twelve cartons with their physical count of eleven. They agree on eleven present cartons. The contents have not been checked, and dispatch has not located a twelfth carton. The difference is one carton against the listed quantity, not a verified count of missing individual products. They need a factual discrepancy report and a dispatch follow-up, not a guess about where the carton was lost.
Language workshop: Twelve on paper, eleven at receiving
Communication goal: Compare document and physical quantities, distinguish cartons from contents, and report an unresolved shortage without assigning blame.
Language guidance: Twelve listed cartons minus eleven counted cartons gives a one-carton discrepancy. That calculation says nothing about the number of individual products missing or the contents of the cartons present, because no contents check has occurred.
Useful expressions (preserve the supplied facts): The bill of lading lists twelve cartons. / We have counted eleven cartons here. / That is one carton short of the listed quantity. / I mean cartons, not individual products. / Do you also have eleven in your count? / We have not checked the contents. / Dispatch has not located the twelfth carton. / We do not yet know the cause of the difference. / Please record twelve listed and eleven counted. / Can dispatch investigate the unlocated carton? / I cannot say it is at another depot. / Eleven cartons does not mean eleven products. / Twelve comes from the document; eleven comes from our count. / A trace request is not a confirmed location. / One carton is unaccounted for against the listed quantity. / The carton discrepancy remains unresolved.
Vocabulary: bill of lading: Transport document recording shipment details and carriage terms, often BOL. / carton: Individual outer box counted in this shipment. / piece count: Number of stated shipping units, with the unit defined. / listed quantity: Amount recorded on the relevant document. / physical count: Count of units actually observed. / shortage: Quantity below the expected or documented amount. / discrepancy: Difference between records or observations requiring clarification. / recount: A further count to check the original figure. / shipping unit: Unit used for shipment counting, such as a carton or pallet. / pallet: Platform used to support and move grouped goods. / packing list: Document describing items packed in a shipment. / line item: Separate entry on a shipment or order document. / contents: Goods inside the outer packaging. / item quantity: Number of individual goods rather than outer packages. / unchecked: Not yet examined or verified. / short by: Expression stating the amount below an expected quantity. / overage: Quantity above the expected or documented amount. / quantity basis: Unit and reference used for a numerical comparison. / receiving tally: Count recorded when goods arrive. / trace request: Request to investigate the location or movement of a shipment. / unlocated carton: Carton whose current location has not been established. / dispatch follow-up: Further action by the delivery coordination team. / joint count: Count checked by two parties together. / cause attribution: Statement assigning a reason or responsibility for an event.
Speaking task: Read twelve cartons listed and eleven cartons counted, then state one carton short. Repeat the contents-unknown line without changing cartons into individual products. Use turns 14 and 20 to keep the shared count separate from the unresolved cause.
Partner: Priya: Driver / Ben: Receiver
Guided response task: Select supplied answers for the dialogue gaps. Check the explanations, then read the completed exchange aloud; no original writing is required.
Optional second-round challenge: At Cedar Supplies, the document lists nine cartons. Driver and receiver count eight. Contents are unchecked, and the ninth carton has no confirmed location.
Model for comparison AFTER my attempt, not a response to give me first: Ben: Can the follow-up use this shipment reference? I don't want it passed on as a query about one individual office-supply item.
Priya: The dispatch follow-up should use the shipment details and the one-carton difference. It must not invent an item count or a location.
Ben: Read-back: twelve cartons listed, eleven counted, contents unchecked, and no confirmed location for carton twelve. Have I missed anything?
Priya: Yes. That preserves the physical count and the limits of our information. The discrepancy remains open until the relevant checks establish more.
END REFERENCE

3. Explain why each choice fits or fails, then ask me to read the completed exchange aloud. Do not request a draft, original written sentence or essay.
4. Repeat with three new exchanges, one at a time, moving from recognition to a more subtle meaning contrast. Do not copy the published checks. Label invented variations and do not turn uncertainty into a promise.

TASK: Interactive workplace role-play

Do not resolve approvals, evidence or commitments that remain uncertain.

Give up to three focused suggestions. Ask me to retry the weakest turn; wait. Only then offer an alternative wording and a harder replay.

03Updating an arrival estimateAn estimate after the cutoff+

Your goal

Compare an estimated arrival with a receiving deadline and commit to a specific update without promising acceptance.

This lesson in the book, page 22 ↗

A Read the situation

An estimate after the cutoff

Driver Omar is safely parked and calls dispatcher Mei at 10:20. His current estimated arrival is 11:40, but the receiver stops accepting this delivery at 11:30. No later slot has been confirmed. Mei can contact receiving and update Omar by 10:40, even if the question remains unresolved. The conversation must preserve the ten-minute mismatch, distinguish the update deadline from arrival, and avoid implying that the driver should rush or exceed any applicable driving or working-time limits.

Omar: Driver / Mei: Dispatcher

Check the facts · 3 questions

Choose the answer supported by the briefing. Do not assume that a proposed action has already happened.

1. When is arrival currently estimated?

Answer and explanation

B. 11:40

The arrival estimate is eleven forty, separate from the call and update times.

2. How does the estimate compare with the cutoff?

Answer and explanation

A. Ten minutes after it

Eleven forty is ten minutes later than the eleven-thirty receiving cutoff.

3. What can Mei commit to?

Answer and explanation

A. An update by 10:40, even if unresolved

Mei can provide a communication update without guaranteeing the receiving decision.

B Find the words

Vocabulary and collocations

At 10:20, the estimate is 11:40 and the cutoff is 11:30: a ten-minute mismatch. By 10:40 is the promised update deadline. None of those facts confirms permission to arrive after the cutoff.

24 field terms and natural word combinations
estimated time of arrival
Provisional expected arrival time, commonly ETA.update the estimated time of arrival
receiving cutoff
Latest stated time for accepting the delivery.confirm the receiving cutoff
delivery window
Time range arranged or expected for a delivery.clarify the delivery window
appointment slot
Specific time allocation that requires confirmation.request an appointment slot
later slot
Receiving time after the original limit or booking.ask about a later slot
time mismatch
Difference between an estimate and an applicable timing constraint.report a time mismatch
status update
Message giving the current position of an issue.provide a status update
update deadline
Latest time promised for the next communication.state the update deadline
by
No later than a stated time.update by 10:40
at
At a particular stated time.call at 10:20
after
Later than a reference time.arrive after the cutoff
before
Earlier than a reference time.contact receiving before arrival
receiving confirmation
Explicit acceptance of the proposed receiving arrangement.obtain receiving confirmation
provisional
Subject to change rather than guaranteed.give a provisional estimate
revised estimate
Updated expectation based on current information.issue a revised estimate
delay notification
Notice that timing may differ from the expected plan.send a delay notification
schedule constraint
Limit affecting whether a proposed time is workable.explain a schedule constraint
on duty
Working status relevant to actual driver rules and records.clarify on-duty status
hours of service
Applicable limits and rest requirements governing covered drivers, often HOS.follow hours-of-service requirements
driver availability
Whether a driver can lawfully and practically perform the proposed work.verify driver availability
pending response
Reply that has not yet been received.record a pending response
rescheduling request
Proposal to change the arranged time, not automatic acceptance.submit a rescheduling request
confirmed appointment
Receiving arrangement explicitly agreed by the appropriate parties.distinguish a confirmed appointment
UTC offset
Difference between local time and Coordinated Universal Time.state the UTC offset

A customer timing request does not override safe driving, actual working-time requirements, or carrier procedures. This lesson practices reporting the timing conflict and obtaining a decision; it supplies no instruction to change speed, rest, or duty records.

C Notice the language

An early warning is useful before there is a solution

A dispatcher needs the current estimate, the receiving constraint, and the next contact time. Report a likely mismatch plainly. Do not hide it inside an optimistic phrase such as nearly on time or turn a request for a later slot into an accepted appointment.

16 useful phrases, grouped by purpose
State the call time
It is 10:20, and I am parked for this call.
Give the estimate
My current estimated arrival is 11:40.
Name the cutoff
Receiving has an 11:30 cutoff.
Quantify the mismatch
That puts the estimate ten minutes after the cutoff.
Avoid minimizing
We should flag the mismatch now.
Keep the estimate provisional
11:40 is an estimate, not a guarantee.
Ask about confirmation
Has receiving agreed to a later slot?
State the open status
No later slot is confirmed yet.
Assign the contact
I will contact receiving about the timing.
Commit to an update
I will update you by 10:40.
Keep the commitment even if unresolved
You will hear from me even if their answer is still pending.
Distinguish the times
10:40 is the update deadline, not the arrival time.
Reject a false appointment
A request for 11:40 is not a confirmed appointment.
Protect the factual record
Keep the 11:30 cutoff in the message.
Avoid pressure to rush
We need a receiving decision, not a promise to make up time.
Close the handoff
I own the receiving call and the next update.
6 language and precision notes
By versus at
By gives a latest communication time; at names a particular time.
Estimated
Marks expected arrival as provisional rather than guaranteed.
Ten minutes after
Quantifies the mismatch without claiming acceptance.
Even if
Keeps the update commitment valid when the decision is unfinished.
Requested versus confirmed
A proposed later slot needs an actual accepting response.
Make up time
Can imply unsafe pressure; clarify the receiving plan instead.

D Check your understanding

Choose the precise message

Choose one best answer using the case facts and the stated purpose.

1. Which update is accurate?

Answer and explanation

A. ETA 11:40; cutoff 11:30; later slot unconfirmed.

The statement retains the estimate, unchanged cutoff, and unconfirmed later arrangement.

2. What does by 10:40 refer to?

Answer and explanation

B. The deadline for Mei's update

Ten forty is a communication deadline, not a movement or delivery instruction.

3. Which sentence turns a request into a false result?

Answer and explanation

D. I will ask, so your 11:40 appointment is confirmed.

Asking receiving does not establish that receiving has accepted a later appointment.

4. What should Omar do linguistically?

Answer and explanation

B. Report the timing conflict without promising to rush.

The useful message states the conflict and avoids unsafe or unsupported timing promises.

E Conversations

An estimate after the cutoff

Extended conversation · 20 turns · 10 gaps

Omar: Driver / Mei: Dispatcher

Complete the numbered gaps. Use each bank entry once, without changing its form.

Word bank
  • update deadline
  • rescheduling request
  • status update
  • confirmed appointment
  • by
  • time mismatch
  • receiving cutoff
  • estimated time of arrival
  • driver availability
  • later slot
  1. Omar
    Mei, I'm parked for this call. It's ten twenty. My arrival estimate is eleven forty, but the receiving note looks earlier.
  2. Mei
    Their 1 is eleven thirty. That's ten minutes before your estimate, so we need a receiving decision.
  3. Omar
    That's why I'm calling now. I don't have anything confirming that they'll accept this delivery after eleven thirty.
  4. Mei
    I'll make a 2. For now I'll leave the eleven-thirty cutoff in the record, not replace it with your estimate.
  5. Omar
    Please make clear it's still an estimate. I don't want eleven forty going into their system as a guaranteed arrival.
  6. Mei
    Yes, your 3 is eleven forty. I'll give them that and ask whether a later arrangement is possible.
  7. Omar
    When will you update me? Even a message saying you're still waiting would help me know where things stand.
  8. Mei
    I'll update you 4 ten forty, whether or not they've decided. That's the latest time for my next message.
  9. Omar
    Good. If there's no answer from them, please say that explicitly. I won't read silence as permission for the later arrival.
  10. Mei
    You'll get a 5 either way. If the request is still open, I'll say so.
  11. Omar
    Ten minutes sounds small, but it still puts the estimate beyond their limit. We can't call that within the window.
  12. Mei
    Agreed. The 6 stays in the message. Only the appropriate receiving decision can settle the scheduling question.
  13. Omar
    I also can't promise to make up those minutes on the road. We need a workable receiving plan.
  14. Mei
    Right. I'll keep 7 and the applicable rules in view. This isn't a request to rush or change your duty record.
  15. Omar
    Has anyone else already spoken to them, or is the later time still entirely unconfirmed?
  16. Mei
    No 8 is confirmed. I'll take that contact action and report their actual response.
  17. Omar
    Let me check the times: this call at ten twenty, arrival estimated eleven forty, and your message no later than ten forty.
  18. Mei
    Correct. My 9 is ten forty. The receiving cutoff remains eleven thirty.
  19. Omar
    Thanks. I'll wait for that update without treating eleven forty as a booking. Keep the estimate and their cutoff separate.
  20. Mei
    I will. A 10 still needs an accepted arrangement. I own the contact and the update by ten forty.

Answers and explanations
  1. receiving cutoff Turn 2. The receiving cutoff is 11:30; an 11:40 estimate is ten minutes after it and does not extend it.
  2. rescheduling request Turn 4. A rescheduling request asks for a changed receiving arrangement; it does not itself confirm a new slot.
  3. estimated time of arrival Turn 6. The estimated time of arrival is the provisional 11:40 expectation, not an accepted appointment.
  4. by Turn 8. By means no later than 10:40 for the communication; it says nothing about delivery completion.
  5. status update Turn 10. A status update conveys the latest position even if receiving has not yet decided on the request.
  6. time mismatch Turn 12. The mismatch is the ten minutes between the 11:30 cutoff and the later 11:40 arrival estimate.
  7. driver availability Turn 14. Driver availability includes lawful and practical limits; a requested appointment cannot override applicable rules or safe operation.
  8. later slot Turn 16. No later receiving slot is confirmed; the dispatcher is only taking the contact action.
  9. update deadline Turn 18. 10:40 is the update deadline, separate from the call at 10:20 and the ETA at 11:40.
  10. confirmed appointment Turn 20. A confirmed appointment requires acceptance of a receiving arrangement; asking for it supplies no such acceptance.
Read the complete conversation
  1. Omar: Mei, I'm parked for this call. It's ten twenty. My arrival estimate is eleven forty, but the receiving note looks earlier.
  2. Mei: Their receiving cutoff is eleven thirty. That's ten minutes before your estimate, so we need a receiving decision.
  3. Omar: That's why I'm calling now. I don't have anything confirming that they'll accept this delivery after eleven thirty.
  4. Mei: I'll make a rescheduling request. For now I'll leave the eleven-thirty cutoff in the record, not replace it with your estimate.
  5. Omar: Please make clear it's still an estimate. I don't want eleven forty going into their system as a guaranteed arrival.
  6. Mei: Yes, your estimated time of arrival is eleven forty. I'll give them that and ask whether a later arrangement is possible.
  7. Omar: When will you update me? Even a message saying you're still waiting would help me know where things stand.
  8. Mei: I'll update you by ten forty, whether or not they've decided. That's the latest time for my next message.
  9. Omar: Good. If there's no answer from them, please say that explicitly. I won't read silence as permission for the later arrival.
  10. Mei: You'll get a status update either way. If the request is still open, I'll say so.
  11. Omar: Ten minutes sounds small, but it still puts the estimate beyond their limit. We can't call that within the window.
  12. Mei: Agreed. The time mismatch stays in the message. Only the appropriate receiving decision can settle the scheduling question.
  13. Omar: I also can't promise to make up those minutes on the road. We need a workable receiving plan.
  14. Mei: Right. I'll keep driver availability and the applicable rules in view. This isn't a request to rush or change your duty record.
  15. Omar: Has anyone else already spoken to them, or is the later time still entirely unconfirmed?
  16. Mei: No later slot is confirmed. I'll take that contact action and report their actual response.
  17. Omar: Let me check the times: this call at ten twenty, arrival estimated eleven forty, and your message no later than ten forty.
  18. Mei: Correct. My update deadline is ten forty. The receiving cutoff remains eleven thirty.
  19. Omar: Thanks. I'll wait for that update without treating eleven forty as a booking. Keep the estimate and their cutoff separate.
  20. Mei: I will. A confirmed appointment still needs an accepted arrangement. I own the contact and the update by ten forty.
Dialogue in the book, page 28 ↗
Three more conversations at work

Each short conversation has its own fictional situation and facts.

Ten minutes beyond the cutoff

A parked driver calls dispatch at 10:20 about stop five.

  1. Driver: Stop five's estimated time of arrival is eleven forty, after their eleven thirty cutoff.
  2. Dispatcher: Thanks for calling while parked. Have they offered a later receiving appointment today?
  3. Driver: Not yet. The original delivery window no longer fits the estimate I have.
  4. Dispatcher: I can call receiving now and give you a booking update by ten forty.
  5. Driver: Please distinguish their acceptance decision from my arrival estimate when you speak to them.
  6. Dispatcher: Certainly. I won't say the later arrival automatically means they will unload us.
  7. Driver: If they only offer tomorrow, I'll need you to review the rest of this route.
  8. Dispatcher: I'll include that in the update. For now, no replacement appointment is confirmed.
  9. Driver: So ten forty is our next communication time, not a revised delivery promise.
  10. Dispatcher: Correct. I'll report the actual offer or explain that we're still waiting.
Early does not mean booked

A parked driver discusses an early arrival with a receiver.

  1. Driver: I'm earlier than expected, but my delivery appointment is still scheduled for two thirty.
  2. Receiver: Our receiving team is handling another booking. We can't confirm earlier acceptance today.
  3. Driver: Understood. Should I describe my status to dispatch as arrived, awaiting the booked window?
  4. Receiver: Yes, although check-in also needs confirmation from the receiving desk, not just this call.
  5. Driver: Thanks for distinguishing those. I'm at the site, but receiving hasn't checked me in.
  6. Receiver: Exactly. Your appointment has not moved simply because your journey took less time.
  7. Driver: I'll tell dispatch the original two-thirty appointment remains in place until receiving confirms otherwise.
  8. Receiver: That will prevent them from telling the next customer you've already finished here.
  9. Driver: Please let the receiving desk know I'm asking about status, not demanding immediate service.
  10. Receiver: I can pass that on. They will confirm your check-in and any available change.
A range instead of a sharp promise

A driver calls a customer from a parked vehicle about an uncertain arrival.

  1. Driver: The latest estimate is between one fifteen and one forty-five for your delivery.
  2. Customer: I heard one fifteen earlier and arranged for someone to arrive at exactly then.
  3. Driver: I'm sorry; I gave the lower end as though it were the whole estimate.
  4. Customer: Does that mean you're now promising one forty-five instead, or is that uncertain too?
  5. Driver: That's still an estimate, not a guaranteed latest arrival. Dispatch is tracking the delay.
  6. Customer: I need to tell my colleague what we can actually rely on today.
  7. Driver: You can tell them the current estimated range and that dispatch will update you at noon.
  8. Customer: All right. Please make sure noon refers to the update, not the delivery.
  9. Driver: It does. I'll ask dispatch to use the full range in that message.
  10. Customer: Thank you. I'll pass along the range instead of another exact arrival promise.

F Say it

Two scenarios to practice

An estimate after the cutoff
  1. Read the four times in the case: call 10:20, ETA 11:40, cutoff 11:30, update by 10:40.
  2. Repeat the by-10:40 commitment and stress that an unresolved answer still gets an update.

On your own: read both roles. With a partner: swap roles after the first reading. Use the completed conversation to check any line you need to repeat.

Separate another estimate and update

A parked driver calls at 13:00. Arrival is estimated at 14:20 and receiving closes for this delivery at 14:00. No later slot is agreed. Dispatcher Jo promises an update by 13:15.

Choose one reply for each gap, then read the four turns in order. Check the explanation after each choice.

1. Driver: "Estimated arrival is ___."

Answer and explanation

D. 14:20

Fourteen twenty is the provisional arrival time, not a confirmed appointment.

2. Dispatcher: "That is ___ minutes after the cutoff."

Answer and explanation

A. twenty

Fourteen twenty follows the fourteen-hundred cutoff by twenty minutes.

3. Driver: "Your update is due by ___."

Answer and explanation

C. 13:15

Thirteen fifteen is the promised communication deadline in the new scenario.

4. Dispatcher: "A later slot remains ___."

Answer and explanation

A. unconfirmed

Receiving has not agreed to an alternative delivery time.

Read the completed exchange twice, switching roles. Keep the printed facts and professional responsibilities unchanged.
