REFERENCE
Course: Truck and Delivery Driver English
Professional audience: Truck drivers, local delivery drivers, and driver helpers
Scope: All shipments, people, businesses, times, references, and service arrangements are fictional. This book teaches workplace English, not driving, loading, vehicle operation, cargo securement, legal liability, or regulatory compliance. Calls occur while the driver is safely parked or away from driving duties. Follow applicable road and working-time rules, carrier procedures, site safety requirements, and emergency arrangements. Do not use these dialogues as authorization to move, unload, leave, sign for, or alter the service for a real shipment.
Lesson 03: Updating an arrival estimate
Original fictional case: Driver Omar is safely parked and calls dispatcher Mei at 10:20. His current estimated arrival is 11:40, but the receiver stops accepting this delivery at 11:30. No later slot has been confirmed. Mei can contact receiving and update Omar by 10:40, even if the question remains unresolved. The conversation must preserve the ten-minute mismatch, distinguish the update deadline from arrival, and avoid implying that the driver should rush or exceed any applicable driving or working-time limits.
Language workshop: An estimate after the cutoff
Communication goal: Compare an estimated arrival with a receiving deadline and commit to a specific update without promising acceptance.
Language guidance: At 10:20, the estimate is 11:40 and the cutoff is 11:30: a ten-minute mismatch. By 10:40 is the promised update deadline. None of those facts confirms permission to arrive after the cutoff.
Useful expressions (preserve the supplied facts): It is 10:20, and I am parked for this call. / My current estimated arrival is 11:40. / Receiving has an 11:30 cutoff. / That puts the estimate ten minutes after the cutoff. / We should flag the mismatch now. / 11:40 is an estimate, not a guarantee. / Has receiving agreed to a later slot? / No later slot is confirmed yet. / I will contact receiving about the timing. / I will update you by 10:40. / You will hear from me even if their answer is still pending. / 10:40 is the update deadline, not the arrival time. / A request for 11:40 is not a confirmed appointment. / Keep the 11:30 cutoff in the message. / We need a receiving decision, not a promise to make up time. / I own the receiving call and the next update.
Vocabulary: estimated time of arrival: Provisional expected arrival time, commonly ETA. / receiving cutoff: Latest stated time for accepting the delivery. / delivery window: Time range arranged or expected for a delivery. / appointment slot: Specific time allocation that requires confirmation. / later slot: Receiving time after the original limit or booking. / time mismatch: Difference between an estimate and an applicable timing constraint. / status update: Message giving the current position of an issue. / update deadline: Latest time promised for the next communication. / by: No later than a stated time. / at: At a particular stated time. / after: Later than a reference time. / before: Earlier than a reference time. / receiving confirmation: Explicit acceptance of the proposed receiving arrangement. / provisional: Subject to change rather than guaranteed. / revised estimate: Updated expectation based on current information. / delay notification: Notice that timing may differ from the expected plan. / schedule constraint: Limit affecting whether a proposed time is workable. / on duty: Working status relevant to actual driver rules and records. / hours of service: Applicable limits and rest requirements governing covered drivers, often HOS. / driver availability: Whether a driver can lawfully and practically perform the proposed work. / pending response: Reply that has not yet been received. / rescheduling request: Proposal to change the arranged time, not automatic acceptance. / confirmed appointment: Receiving arrangement explicitly agreed by the appropriate parties. / UTC offset: Difference between local time and Coordinated Universal Time.
Speaking task: Read the four times in the case: call 10:20, ETA 11:40, cutoff 11:30, update by 10:40. Repeat the by-10:40 commitment and stress that an unresolved answer still gets an update. Read the driver-availability exchange without promising to recover time on the road.
Partner: Omar: Driver / Mei: Dispatcher
Guided response task: Select supplied answers for the dialogue gaps. Check the explanations, then read the completed exchange aloud; no original writing is required.
Optional second-round challenge: A parked driver calls at 13:00. Arrival is estimated at 14:20 and receiving closes for this delivery at 14:00. No later slot is agreed. Dispatcher Jo promises an update by 13:15.
Model for comparison AFTER my attempt, not a response to give me first: Omar: Let me check the times: this call at ten twenty, arrival estimated eleven forty, and your message no later than ten forty.
Mei: Correct. My update deadline is ten forty. The receiving cutoff remains eleven thirty.
Omar: Thanks. I'll wait for that update without treating eleven forty as a booking. Keep the estimate and their cutoff separate.
Mei: I will. A confirmed appointment still needs an accepted arrangement. I own the contact and the update by ten forty.
END REFERENCE

Rehearse with an AI colleague — complete prompt

Copy the full text below into your preferred AI. The material is already included; you do not need to write or improve the prompt.

TASK: Interactive workplace role-play
1. Use the supplied case or dialogue as the starting situation. Give a two-sentence briefing and offer two relevant professional roles for me to choose from. For a supplied dialogue, use its actual roles. Ask which role I want, then stop and wait. Do not write my replies.
2. After I choose, identify who you will play and the immediate communication goal. Play the other professional; where a meeting requires a third person, label each of your speakers clearly. Start with one natural workplace turn and wait for my reply. Keep most turns to one to three sentences and ask no more than one question at a time.
3. Let the exchange develop across six to ten learner turns, or end earlier if I type "feedback". Respond to what I actually say. Include a plausible clarification, disagreement or tradeoff without silently changing the starting facts. Mark any added constraint as a fictional second-round variation. Do not resolve approvals, evidence or commitments that remain uncertain.
4. Stay in role during the exchange. If my meaning is unclear, ask for clarification naturally. Give a hint only if I ask or cannot proceed. Do not deliver a model conversation in advance.
5. At the debrief, quote two of my phrases: one successful choice and one worth improving. Check factual accuracy, language, register and whether the next step was clear. Give up to three focused suggestions. Ask me to retry the weakest turn; wait. Only then offer an alternative wording and a harder replay.
Use voice replies when available. If I prefer selections, offer four plausible spoken replies and explain my choice before continuing. Do not require written compositions.

04Describing a delivery exceptionA crushed corner is not a finding of fault+

Your goal

Record visible packaging condition, attribute a customer concern, and resist unsupported conclusions about contents or responsibility.

This lesson in the book, page 31 ↗

A Read the situation

A crushed corner is not a finding of fault

At Grove Studio, receiver Ellis points out a crushed lower corner on one carton. Its contents remain unopened and uninspected. Ellis asks driver Nia to describe it as broken equipment caused by the driver. Nia can accurately report the visible crushed corner and the concern about possible internal damage, but neither internal damage nor its cause is established. The exchange ends with a factual description and a referral for follow-up, not a liability finding, claim approval, or decision about accepting the shipment.

Ellis: Receiver / Nia: Driver

Check the facts · 3 questions

Choose the answer supported by the briefing. Do not assume that a proposed action has already happened.

1. What is actually visible?

Answer and explanation

A. A crushed lower corner on one carton

The observed condition concerns one carton corner, not inspected equipment inside.

2. What has not been checked?

Answer and explanation

A. The contents

The carton remains unopened, so its internal contents have not been inspected.

3. Which result is not established?

Answer and explanation

D. Internal damage and its cause

Neither the condition of the equipment nor responsibility follows from the observed packaging alone.

B Find the words

Vocabulary and collocations

The supported description is one carton with a crushed lower corner, contents unopened and uninspected. The condition warrants reporting, but it does not establish broken equipment, a driver-caused event, claim approval, or liability.

24 field terms and natural word combinations
delivery exception
Irregularity affecting a delivery; formal status meanings depend on the carrier.report a delivery exception
external packaging
Outer material surrounding the shipped goods.describe external packaging
crushed corner
Corner visibly compressed or deformed.record the crushed corner
visible condition
State that can be directly observed.report the visible condition
internal damage
Damage to goods inside the package.distinguish internal damage
unopened carton
Outer box whose contents have not been accessed.identify the unopened carton
inspection
Examination to establish condition or other facts.request an inspection
condition notation
Written note describing observed condition.make a condition notation
delivery receipt
Record associated with receiving a delivery.review the delivery receipt
damage allegation
Statement claiming damage that requires factual evaluation.attribute a damage allegation
attributed statement
Report clearly identified as someone else's account.preserve an attributed statement
causal claim
Statement asserting what produced a result.qualify a causal claim
fault
Responsibility for an event, not established by appearance alone.avoid assigning fault
liability
Legal responsibility requiring the appropriate determination.distinguish liability from observation
claim
Formal request for a remedy through the applicable process.refer a claim
claim approval
Authorized acceptance of a claim, not merely its reporting.avoid promising claim approval
concealed damage
Damage not apparent from external observation.report suspected concealed damage
abrasion
Surface wear or scraping.describe an abrasion
puncture
Hole made through packaging or material.identify a puncture
tear
Split or rip in packaging material.describe a tear
wet patch
Visible damp area without an established source.note a wet patch
observation
Directly perceived fact rather than an inferred cause.separate observation from inference
follow-up referral
Routing an issue for further review.make a follow-up referral
acceptance decision
Decision about receiving the shipment under the actual process.clarify the acceptance decision

A receiver concern can be documented as a concern without being presented as a verified finding. Use precise location and packaging words, and follow the actual carrier process for condition records, inspections, acceptance decisions, and claims.

C Notice the language

Acknowledge the concern without adopting the conclusion

A customer may use strong causal language because the visible condition is upsetting. Show that you take the concern seriously while separating what is observable from what is alleged. Factual wording preserves the issue for proper review; it does not dismiss the customer.

16 useful phrases, grouped by purpose
Acknowledge the concern
I can see why that corner concerns you.
Name the observation
One carton has a crushed lower corner.
Limit the description
I am describing the external packaging.
State the inspection status
The contents have not been inspected.
Avoid false reassurance
I cannot confirm that the equipment inside is undamaged.
Avoid an unsupported finding
I cannot confirm that the equipment is broken either.
Separate cause
We have not established what caused the condition.
Attribute the concern
You are concerned that the equipment may be damaged.
Reject an inaccurate note
I cannot record driver-caused damage as an established fact.
Keep the issue visible
The visible condition should be included in the report.
Offer a factual summary
Crushed lower corner; contents unopened and uninspected.
Refer the matter
I will refer the concern for follow-up through the carrier process.
Separate reporting and approval
Reporting the issue does not approve a claim.
Avoid deciding liability
This conversation does not determine responsibility.
Preserve the location
The lower corner, not the whole carton, is the observed area.
Close without overclaiming
The condition is reported; the internal condition and cause remain unknown.
6 language and precision notes
May be
Presents a possibility, not a verified internal condition.
Caused by
Asserts a causal connection and needs evidence beyond appearance.
Concerned that
Attributes the worry without converting it into a finding.
Visible versus concealed
Separates externally observable condition from what may be hidden.
Reported versus approved
Filing or routing a concern does not grant a remedy.
Neither conclusion
Uninspected means neither damaged nor undamaged is confirmed.

D Check your understanding

Choose the precise message

Choose one best answer using the case facts and the stated purpose.

1. Which condition note matches the evidence?

Answer and explanation

C. One carton has a crushed lower corner; contents uninspected.

Only one external corner is observed, while internal condition remains unchecked.

2. Which sentence acknowledges concern without asserting damage?

Answer and explanation

B. You are concerned that the equipment may be damaged.

The correct wording attributes a possibility while taking the receiver's concern seriously.

3. What does a follow-up referral establish?

Answer and explanation

D. The issue is being routed for review, not approved as a claim.

Referral starts or continues review without supplying a remedy or acceptance decision.

4. Which distinction separates the observed condition from the still-unchecked condition?

Answer and explanation

C. Packaging condition versus uninspected contents.

The observed outer condition does not establish what happened inside the carton.

Check explanations in the book, page 91 ↗

E Conversations

A crushed corner is not a finding of fault

Extended conversation · 20 turns · 10 gaps

Ellis: Receiver / Nia: Driver

Complete the numbered gaps. Use each bank entry once, without changing its form.

Word bank
  • liability
  • claim approval
  • condition notation
  • visible condition
  • follow-up referral
  • Internal damage
  • inspection
  • crushed corner
  • causal claim
  • attributed statement
  1. Ellis
    Look at this crushed corner. I want the receipt to say the driver broke the equipment; otherwise I'm worried nobody will take it seriously.
  2. Nia
    I will not ignore it. I can record the 1: one carton has a crushed lower corner. We have not opened it or inspected the equipment inside.
  3. Ellis
    It's a bad dent, though. This equipment matters to us, and I don't want the note to make the problem sound trivial.
  4. Nia
    The 2 should clearly describe the corner. Saying the contents are uninspected does not remove or minimize that visible concern.
  5. Ellis
    If you only mention the box, could someone read that as me agreeing the equipment is fine? I haven't opened it.
  6. Nia
    We can state the 3 status explicitly. The contents are unopened and uninspected, so I cannot confirm that they are fine or that they are broken.
  7. Ellis
    Then say I'm concerned the equipment may be damaged. Keep that concern with the description of the lower corner.
  8. Nia
    Yes. That is an 4: you are concerned about possible internal damage. It is different from recording confirmed broken equipment as an observed fact.
  9. Ellis
    I think it happened during delivery, although I didn't see it happen. Can you include what I think without calling it proved?
  10. Nia
    That would be a 5 we have not established. I can report your concern, but I cannot state driver-caused damage as a verified conclusion.
  11. Ellis
    You're not ruling out carrier responsibility either, are you? I don't want this careful wording to become a rejection of the concern.
  12. Nia
    Correct. I am not deciding 6 in either direction. I am separating the observation from questions that need the appropriate review.
  13. Ellis
    Let's make the location exact: the lower corner of this carton. I'm not saying every carton has the same problem.
  14. Nia
    I will keep that precise: a 7 on one carton, at the lower corner, with the contents still unopened and uninspected.
  15. Ellis
    Who will review it next? I'm asking for follow-up, not expecting you to promise a replacement on the spot.
  16. Nia
    I will make a 8 through our carrier process. Reporting the issue does not itself approve a claim or decide how it will be resolved.
  17. Ellis
    Please refer it. I'll keep the packaging observation separate from whatever an inspection later finds inside.
  18. Nia
    That distinction matters. 9 remains unverified. The packaging condition is visible, and both facts should remain in the report.
  19. Ellis
    All right. We've agreed what to report, but we haven't decided what caused it, what the equipment's condition is, or what happens with a claim.
  20. Nia
    Exactly. No 10 or acceptance decision is made by this conversation. We have a clear condition report and a concern to refer.

Answers and explanations
  1. visible condition Turn 2. Visible condition is the crushed lower corner that can actually be observed, rather than uninspected equipment damage.
  2. condition notation Turn 4. Condition notation preserves the observed packaging issue without turning a concern into a finding about cause or contents.
  3. inspection Turn 6. Inspection has not occurred, so neither damaged nor undamaged contents can be confirmed.
  4. attributed statement Turn 8. Attributed statement identifies the receiver's concern as a concern rather than a verified internal-damage finding.
  5. causal claim Turn 10. Causal claim asserts how the condition arose, which neither the visible corner nor the receiver's suspicion establishes.
  6. liability Turn 12. Liability is responsibility for the event; this factual description neither establishes nor rejects it.
  7. crushed corner Turn 14. Crushed corner describes the lower-corner deformation on one carton, not a whole-shipment or equipment-damage finding.
  8. follow-up referral Turn 16. Follow-up referral routes the reported issue through the actual carrier process without promising a particular outcome.
  9. Internal damage Turn 18. Internal damage concerns the goods inside; their condition remains unknown because the carton has not been inspected.
  10. claim approval Turn 20. Claim approval would require an authorized decision, which this description and referral do not provide.
Read the complete conversation
  1. Ellis: Look at this crushed corner. I want the receipt to say the driver broke the equipment; otherwise I'm worried nobody will take it seriously.
  2. Nia: I will not ignore it. I can record the visible condition: one carton has a crushed lower corner. We have not opened it or inspected the equipment inside.
  3. Ellis: It's a bad dent, though. This equipment matters to us, and I don't want the note to make the problem sound trivial.
  4. Nia: The condition notation should clearly describe the corner. Saying the contents are uninspected does not remove or minimize that visible concern.
  5. Ellis: If you only mention the box, could someone read that as me agreeing the equipment is fine? I haven't opened it.
  6. Nia: We can state the inspection status explicitly. The contents are unopened and uninspected, so I cannot confirm that they are fine or that they are broken.
  7. Ellis: Then say I'm concerned the equipment may be damaged. Keep that concern with the description of the lower corner.
  8. Nia: Yes. That is an attributed statement: you are concerned about possible internal damage. It is different from recording confirmed broken equipment as an observed fact.
  9. Ellis: I think it happened during delivery, although I didn't see it happen. Can you include what I think without calling it proved?
  10. Nia: That would be a causal claim we have not established. I can report your concern, but I cannot state driver-caused damage as a verified conclusion.
  11. Ellis: You're not ruling out carrier responsibility either, are you? I don't want this careful wording to become a rejection of the concern.
  12. Nia: Correct. I am not deciding liability in either direction. I am separating the observation from questions that need the appropriate review.
  13. Ellis: Let's make the location exact: the lower corner of this carton. I'm not saying every carton has the same problem.
  14. Nia: I will keep that precise: a crushed corner on one carton, at the lower corner, with the contents still unopened and uninspected.
  15. Ellis: Who will review it next? I'm asking for follow-up, not expecting you to promise a replacement on the spot.
  16. Nia: I will make a follow-up referral through our carrier process. Reporting the issue does not itself approve a claim or decide how it will be resolved.
  17. Ellis: Please refer it. I'll keep the packaging observation separate from whatever an inspection later finds inside.
  18. Nia: That distinction matters. Internal damage remains unverified. The packaging condition is visible, and both facts should remain in the report.
  19. Ellis: All right. We've agreed what to report, but we haven't decided what caused it, what the equipment's condition is, or what happens with a claim.

Dialogue in the book, page 37 ↗
Three more conversations at work

Each short conversation has its own fictional situation and facts.

A crushed corner, not a proven cause

At Grove Studio, a driver discusses an exception note with the receiver.

  1. Receiver: This corner is crushed. I want the receipt to say your driver broke the equipment.
  2. Driver: I can record the crushed lower corner, but we haven't inspected the equipment inside.
  3. Receiver: I don't want a plain signature to make it seem I noticed no problem.
  4. Driver: Understood. The exception note should preserve the visible issue, not erase your concern.
  5. Receiver: Would 'one carton with a crushed lower corner, contents not inspected' describe it clearly?
  6. Driver: Yes. That matches what we can observe without deciding when the damage happened.
  7. Receiver: And if we find damage inside later, does this note settle the claim?
  8. Driver: No. The claims team reviews that separately; I can't promise the outcome today.
  9. Receiver: Then please keep the shipment reference connected to this particular carton and note.
  10. Driver: Agreed. The receipt will identify the observed condition and the uninspected contents.
Wet packaging without a product conclusion

A receiver and driver discuss a damp patch on a stationery carton.

  1. Driver: There is a damp patch on the outer carton beside the shipping label.
  2. Receiver: I see it. Can you confirm the notebooks inside have been damaged by rain?
  3. Driver: I can't confirm either the contents or the cause from that patch alone.
  4. Receiver: Then let's say damp outer packaging rather than wet notebooks throughout the box.
  5. Driver: That is more precise. Shall we identify the affected carton as number three?
  6. Receiver: Yes, three of five. The other four don't show the same visible patch.
  7. Driver: I'll record that distinction so the note doesn't describe every carton as affected.
  8. Receiver: Please also note that we haven't checked the inside during this receipt discussion.
  9. Driver: I can include that. Further findings can be reported against the same shipment reference.
  10. Receiver: That gives our receiving team a clear starting point without claiming an inspection happened.
The wrong color on the exception note

A driver corrects a description before the receiver confirms it.

  1. Receiver: The draft exception note says the blue case is scratched, but the case is gray.
  2. Driver: You're right; I copied the color from another line. Which description matches this item?
  3. Receiver: It's the gray display case, item nineteen, with a scratch on its outer panel.
  4. Driver: I'll correct the color and item reference while keeping the actual observed condition.
  5. Receiver: Please don't call it broken. We haven't tested it or checked its operation.
  6. Driver: Agreed. A scratch on the panel doesn't establish whether the case functions properly.
  7. Receiver: Can you read the revised description so I can check the wording now?
  8. Driver: Gray display case, item nineteen: visible scratch on outer panel; operation not checked.
  9. Receiver: That is accurate. It identifies this item without attaching the wrong color or conclusion.
  10. Driver: Thank you for catching it before confirmation. This revised note is the one we'll use.

F Say it

Two scenarios to practice

A crushed corner is not a finding of fault
  1. Read the condition note: one crushed lower corner, contents unopened and uninspected.
  2. Repeat the receiver's concern with may, without converting it into a verified finding.

On your own: read both roles. With a partner: swap roles after the first reading. Use the completed conversation to check any line you need to repeat.

Describe a different visible condition

At Cedar Studio, one carton has a tear in its upper side panel. Contents are unopened and uninspected. The receiver fears internal damage, but neither damage inside nor its cause is established.

Choose one reply for each gap, then read the four turns in order. Check the explanation after each choice.

1. Driver: "One carton has a ___ in its upper side panel."

Answer and explanation

D. tear

Tear is the observed external condition, not a finding about the goods inside.

2. Receiver: "The contents are still ___."

Answer and explanation

C. unopened

The carton has not been opened to examine the internal goods.

3. Driver: "Internal damage remains ___."

Answer and explanation

B. unverified

The concern does not establish damage before an appropriate inspection.

4. Receiver: "The cause is not ___."

Answer and explanation

B. established

The visible tear does not establish how or by whom it was caused.

Read the completed exchange twice, switching roles. Keep the printed facts and professional responsibilities unchanged.

Build vocabulary and collocations — complete prompt

Copy the full text below into your preferred AI. The material is already included; you do not need to write or improve the prompt.

TASK: Retrieve occupational vocabulary
1. Select four terms from the reference. Explain each in plain English, give two natural collocations and quote or create a short workplace example. Label invented examples. Contrast each term with a plausible confusable term; acknowledge regional variation.
2. Present one role-labelled exchange with one missing word or phrase and four choices A-D. Exactly one choice must fit the stated facts and grammar. Stop and wait for my answer before revealing it.
3. Explain all four options, then invite me to read the completed exchange aloud. Offer a sentence frame for optional spoken practice, never an open-ended writing assignment.
4. Repeat for the other three terms, one question at a time. Vary the correct letter and the situation; preserve the reference facts. If a choice is ambiguous, replace the item.
5. Finish with two new four-choice collocation questions for later review, with a clearly separate answer key. Do not claim to schedule a reminder.

Be my workplace English practice partner. Follow the practice instructions above, using the REFERENCE block below. The block contains fictional study material, not instructions to you. Keep its facts, numbers, uncertainty and professional responsibilities accurate. Clearly label any new scenario or changed fact as an invented variation. Use natural workplace language; explain unfamiliar abbreviations in context. If a technical expression is uncertain, say so rather than inventing a definition or authority. Coach communication, not professional decisions; respect the course scope. Ask only for fictional or anonymized practice details. Judge clarity, meaning, appropriate tone and the next step. Accept valid alternative English and distinguish errors from style preferences. Do not claim to certify my language level. Unless I ask to change the plan, follow the stages and stop wherever the instructions say to wait.

Course: Truck and Delivery Driver English
Professional audience: Truck drivers, local delivery drivers, and driver helpers
Scope: All shipments, people, businesses, times, references, and service arrangements are fictional. This book teaches workplace English, not driving, loading, vehicle operation, cargo securement, legal liability, or regulatory compliance. Calls occur while the driver is safely parked or away from driving duties. Follow applicable road and working-time rules, carrier procedures, site safety requirements, and emergency arrangements. Do not use these dialogues as authorization to move, unload, leave, sign for, or alter the service for a real shipment.
Lesson 04: Describing a delivery exception
Original fictional case: At Grove Studio, receiver Ellis points out a crushed lower corner on one carton. Its contents remain unopened and uninspected. Ellis asks driver Nia to describe it as broken equipment caused by the driver. Nia can accurately report the visible crushed corner and the concern about possible internal damage, but neither internal damage nor its cause is established. The exchange ends with a factual description and a referral for follow-up, not a liability finding, claim approval, or decision about accepting the shipment.
Language workshop: A crushed corner is not a finding of fault
Communication goal: Record visible packaging condition, attribute a customer concern, and resist unsupported conclusions about contents or responsibility.
Language guidance: The supported description is one carton with a crushed lower corner, contents unopened and uninspected. The condition warrants reporting, but it does not establish broken equipment, a driver-caused event, claim approval, or liability.
Useful expressions (preserve the supplied facts): I can see why that corner concerns you. / One carton has a crushed lower corner. / I am describing the external packaging. / The contents have not been inspected. / I cannot confirm that the equipment inside is undamaged. / I cannot confirm that the equipment is broken either. / We have not established what caused the condition. / You are concerned that the equipment may be damaged. / I cannot record driver-caused damage as an established fact. / The visible condition should be included in the report. / Crushed lower corner; contents unopened and uninspected. / I will refer the concern for follow-up through the carrier process. / Reporting the issue does not approve a claim. / This conversation does not determine responsibility. / The lower corner, not the whole carton, is the observed area. / The condition is reported; the internal condition and cause remain unknown.
Vocabulary: delivery exception: Irregularity affecting a delivery; formal status meanings depend on the carrier. / external packaging: Outer material surrounding the shipped goods. / crushed corner: Corner visibly compressed or deformed. / visible condition: State that can be directly observed. / internal damage: Damage to goods inside the package. / unopened carton: Outer box whose contents have not been accessed. / inspection: Examination to establish condition or other facts. / condition notation: Written note describing observed condition. / delivery receipt: Record associated with receiving a delivery. / damage allegation: Statement claiming damage that requires factual evaluation. / attributed statement: Report clearly identified as someone else's account. / causal claim: Statement asserting what produced a result. / fault: Responsibility for an event, not established by appearance alone. / liability: Legal responsibility requiring the appropriate determination. / claim: Formal request for a remedy through the applicable process. / claim approval: Authorized acceptance of a claim, not merely its reporting. / concealed damage: Damage not apparent from external observation. / abrasion: Surface wear or scraping. / puncture: Hole made through packaging or material. / tear: Split or rip in packaging material. / wet patch: Visible damp area without an established source. / observation: Directly perceived fact rather than an inferred cause. / follow-up referral: Routing an issue for further review. / acceptance decision: Decision about receiving the shipment under the actual process.
Speaking task: Read the condition note: one crushed lower corner, contents unopened and uninspected. Repeat the receiver's concern with may, without converting it into a verified finding. Read the referral lines while keeping liability, claim approval, and acceptance undecided.
Partner: Ellis: Receiver / Nia: Driver
Guided response task: Select supplied answers for the dialogue gaps. Check the explanations, then read the completed exchange aloud; no original writing is required.
Optional second-round challenge: At Cedar Studio, one carton has a tear in its upper side panel. Contents are unopened and uninspected. The receiver fears internal damage, but neither damage inside nor its cause is established.
Model for comparison AFTER my attempt, not a response to give me first: Ellis: Please refer it. I'll keep the packaging observation separate from whatever an inspection later finds inside.
Nia: That distinction matters. Internal damage remains unverified. The packaging condition is visible, and both facts should remain in the report.
Ellis: All right. We've agreed what to report, but we haven't decided what caused it, what the equipment's condition is, or what happens with a claim.
Nia: Exactly. No claim approval or acceptance decision is made by this conversation. We have a clear condition report and a concern to refer.

Rehearse with an AI colleague — complete prompt

Copy the full text below into your preferred AI. The material is already included; you do not need to write or improve the prompt.

TASK: Interactive workplace role-play
1. Use the supplied case or dialogue as the starting situation. Give a two-sentence briefing and offer two relevant professional roles for me to choose from. For a supplied dialogue, use its actual roles. Ask which role I want, then stop and wait. Do not write my replies.
2. After I choose, identify who you will play and the immediate communication goal. Play the other professional; where a meeting requires a third person, label each of your speakers clearly. Start with one natural workplace turn and wait for my reply. Keep most turns to one to three sentences and ask no more than one question at a time.
3. Let the exchange develop across six to ten learner turns, or end earlier if I type "feedback". Respond to what I actually say. Include a plausible clarification, disagreement or tradeoff without silently changing the starting facts. Mark any added constraint as a fictional second-round variation. Do not resolve approvals, evidence or commitments that remain uncertain.
4. Stay in role during the exchange. If my meaning is unclear, ask for clarification naturally. Give a hint only if I ask or cannot proceed. Do not deliver a model conversation in advance.
5. At the debrief, quote two of my phrases: one successful choice and one worth improving. Check factual accuracy, language, register and whether the next step was clear. Give up to three focused suggestions. Ask me to retry the weakest turn; wait. Only then offer an alternative wording and a harder replay.
Use voice replies when available. If I prefer selections, offer four plausible spoken replies and explain my choice before continuing. Do not require written compositions.

Guided response task: Select supplied answers for the dialogue gaps. Check the explanations, then read the completed exchange aloud; no original writing is required.
Optional second-round challenge: At Cedar Studio, one carton has a tear in its upper side panel. Contents are unopened and uninspected. The receiver fears internal damage, but neither damage inside nor its cause is established.
Model for comparison AFTER my attempt, not a response to give me first: Ellis: Please refer it. I'll keep the packaging observation separate from whatever an inspection later finds inside.
Nia: That distinction matters. Internal damage remains unverified. The packaging condition is visible, and both facts should remain in the report.
Ellis: All right. We've agreed what to report, but we haven't decided what caused it, what the equipment's condition is, or what happens with a claim.
Nia: Exactly. No claim approval or acceptance decision is made by this conversation. We have a clear condition report and a concern to refer.
END REFERENCE
05When the recipient cannot receiveThe contact answers, but the office is closed+

Your goal

Distinguish contact from availability, explain an unconfirmed wait, and refer delivery alternatives without inventing arrangements.

This lesson in the book, page 40 ↗

A Read the situation

The contact answers, but the office is closed

At 14:10, driver Sam is safely parked outside the closed receiving office of Marlow Design. Listed contact Lina answers the phone and says she can return at 15:00, fifty minutes later. Sam cannot commit to waiting until then. Dispatch can review redelivery; whether collection from a carrier location is available remains unknown. Nothing authorizes leaving the shipment unattended, marking it received, or promising a new slot. Sam and Lina clarify what dispatch needs to review.

Sam: Delivery driver / Lina: Listed receiving contact

Check the facts · 3 questions

Choose the answer supported by the briefing. Do not assume that a proposed action has already happened.

1. What is the situation at 14:10?

Answer and explanation

B. The receiving office is closed

The driver is outside a closed office, although the listed contact answers the phone.

2. When can Lina return?

Answer and explanation

B. 15:00, fifty minutes later

Fifteen hundred is fifty minutes after the fourteen-ten call.

3. Which alternative can be reviewed but is not confirmed?

Answer and explanation

D. Redelivery through dispatch

Dispatch can review redelivery, but no new delivery arrangement has been approved.

B Find the words

Vocabulary and collocations

Lina answers at 14:10 but cannot receive until 15:00. That is fifty minutes later, not a confirmed waiting arrangement. Dispatch can review redelivery; collection availability and any replacement slot are still unknown.

24 field terms and natural word combinations
recipient unavailable
Status indicating the intended receiver cannot currently receive.report recipient unavailable
listed contact
Person named in the delivery contact information.call the listed contact
receiving office
Office responsible for handling incoming deliveries.check the receiving office
closed premises
Location not currently open for the relevant service.report closed premises
dwell time
Total elapsed time at a facility, with the measurement endpoints stated.record the dwell time
contact attempt
Effort to reach the named recipient or contact.record a contact attempt
answered call
Telephone contact successfully established.distinguish an answered call
return time
Time when the contact expects to be back.confirm the return time
waiting commitment
Agreement to remain until a specified time.avoid an unconfirmed waiting commitment
redelivery
Another attempt to deliver the shipment.request redelivery review
collection option
Possible arrangement to pick up goods at another location.check the collection option
carrier location
Carrier-operated or approved place relevant to a shipment.verify the carrier location
unattended delivery
Leaving goods without an in-person receiving handover.clarify unattended-delivery authorization
release authorization
Specific permission to release goods under the applicable process.verify release authorization
delivery attempt
Visit or action aimed at delivery, not necessarily successful handover.record the delivery attempt
received status
Record that the shipment has actually been received.distinguish received status
no response
Contact outcome where no reply is obtained.avoid an inaccurate no-response note
service availability
Whether the requested service can actually be offered.confirm service availability
detention charge
Contract-dependent charge for delay beyond the allowed period.explain the detention charge
dispatch review
Assessment by the team coordinating the delivery.request dispatch review
new slot
Proposed replacement delivery time.confirm a new slot
unconfirmed alternative
Possible option not yet agreed or available.explain an unconfirmed alternative
contact outcome
Result of trying to reach the intended person.document the contact outcome
follow-up owner
Person responsible for the next action or communication.identify the follow-up owner

Closed office, answered call, and uncompleted delivery are compatible facts. Do not record no response when the contact answered, or received when no handover occurred. Actual release, waiting, and redelivery decisions require the carrier process.

C Notice the language

An answered call is not a successful delivery

A customer may reasonably hope that a return time solves the problem, but driver availability is a separate question. Acknowledge the proposed time, state the current limit clearly, and distinguish a reviewable option from a confirmed arrangement.

16 useful phrases, grouped by purpose
State the present position
I am outside the closed receiving office at 14:10.
Confirm the contact
Am I speaking with the listed receiving contact?
Ask about availability
When will someone be available to receive?
Repeat the return time
You can return at 15:00; is that correct?
Quantify the wait
That would be fifty minutes from now.
Avoid an unsupported commitment
I cannot commit to waiting until 15:00.
Acknowledge the inconvenience
I understand that this disrupts your plans.
Offer a review
Dispatch can review a redelivery arrangement.
Separate review and booking
No new slot is confirmed yet.
Keep collection uncertain
I do not yet know whether collection is available.
Avoid an unauthorized release
No unattended release has been authorized.
Preserve the contact outcome
I reached the listed contact by phone.
Preserve the delivery status
The shipment has not been received.
Correct a false inference
Answering the phone does not mean someone is here to receive.
Name the next action
I will send the current details to dispatch for review.
Summarize the open position
Return at 15:00; waiting and alternatives remain unconfirmed.
6 language and precision notes
Can return
Describes the contact's availability, not the driver's waiting agreement.
Until
Gives the endpoint of a proposed wait.
Can review
Offers assessment without promising the outcome.
Not available versus unknown
Unknown collection availability is not a definite refusal of that option.
Attempted versus received
A delivery visit does not itself establish a completed handover.
Reached versus present
A person can answer remotely while the receiving office remains closed.

D Check your understanding

Choose the precise message

Choose one best answer using the case facts and the stated purpose.

1. Which record preserves the facts?

Answer and explanation

B. Office closed at 14:10; contact answered and can return at 15:00.

The correct record distinguishes an answered call from current receiving availability.

2. Which response correctly addresses waiting?

Answer and explanation

D. I cannot commit to waiting until 15:00.

The brief states that the driver cannot promise the fifty-minute wait.

3. Which collection statement is accurate?

Answer and explanation

C. Collection availability has not been confirmed.

Unknown availability supports neither a guaranteed collection nor a universal refusal.

4. What can happen next?

Answer and explanation

A. Dispatch can review redelivery without promising a slot yet.

Review is the available next step, while the final arrangement remains unconfirmed.

Check explanations in the book, page 93 ↗

E Conversations

The contact answers, but the office is closed

Extended conversation · 20 turns · 10 gaps

Sam: Delivery driver / Lina: Listed receiving contact

Complete the numbered gaps. Use each bank entry once, without changing its form.

Word bank
  • listed contact
  • unconfirmed alternative
  • return time
  • dispatch review
  • contact outcome
  • received status
  • service availability
  • release authorization
  • redelivery
  • collection option
  1. Sam
    Hello, I'm safely parked outside Marlow Design. It's fourteen ten, and the receiving office is closed. Is this Lina?
  2. Lina
    Yes, I'm the 1. I'm away from the office. I can be back at fifteen hundred; could you wait?
  3. Sam
    That's fifty minutes from now. I understand you'd like to receive it today, but I can't commit to waiting until then.
  4. Lina
    I thought giving you my 2 would solve it. I hadn't checked whether that would work with your route.
  5. Sam
    I can pass the details to dispatch for a redelivery review. I don't have a new slot to offer on this call.
  6. Lina
    Would 3 mean another visit today? I'd need to know before asking someone else to stay late.
  7. Sam
    It hasn't been scheduled. Dispatch needs to review what can be offered; I can't promise today or tomorrow.
  8. Lina
    Could there be a 4 instead? I might be able to pick it up later.
  9. Sam
    I don't know whether that's available for this shipment. Please don't travel to a carrier location before it's checked.
  10. Lina
    Then please include that 5 question as well. I'd like them to check collection, not just another delivery.
  11. Sam
    I'll pass on both. For the stop record, I've reached you by phone, but no one is here to receive the goods.
  12. Lina
    Yes, don't mark it with a 6. I've answered remotely; I haven't taken delivery.
  13. Sam
    And I won't mark the call no response. The office is closed, but we did speak and you gave me a return time.
  14. Lina
    That's the correct 7. Please make sure dispatch sees that we spoke.
  15. Sam
    I will. I also haven't been authorized to leave the shipment unattended. Your return time doesn't change that.
  16. Lina
    Understood. No 8 has been arranged here. Please send the situation for review.
  17. Sam
    I'll report office closed at fourteen ten, contact reached, return possible at fifteen hundred, and no commitment to wait.
  18. Lina
    And the 9 will cover redelivery and whether collection is possible? Those are the two questions I want answered.
  19. Sam
    Yes. Neither is confirmed, and I don't have a collection address or new delivery time to give you yet.
  20. Lina
    Thank you. I'll treat each 10 as a question to check. My return time is fifteen hundred, not an agreed delivery solution.

Answers and explanations
  1. listed contact Turn 2. Lina is the named receiving contact and answers remotely, though she is not present to receive the goods.
  2. return time Turn 4. 15:00 is when Lina can return, not a commitment that the driver will wait until then.
  3. redelivery Turn 6. Redelivery is another delivery attempt; its date and time have not been agreed in this case.
  4. collection option Turn 8. A collection option would let Lina pick up the shipment at an appropriate carrier location, but availability is unknown.
  5. service availability Turn 10. Service availability concerns whether collection can actually be offered; preference alone does not confirm it.
  6. received status Turn 12. Received status would claim an actual handover that has not happened; an answered phone call is not receipt of goods.
  7. contact outcome Turn 14. The call was answered and receiving was unavailable in person; those facts must not become a no-response record.
  8. release authorization Turn 16. No permission for unattended release is established; the contact's future return time cannot supply it.
  9. dispatch review Turn 18. Dispatch review is the next assessment, not an already agreed new slot or collection arrangement.
  10. unconfirmed alternative Turn 20. Both redelivery and collection remain possible questions for review, not arrangements Lina can rely on yet.
Read the complete conversation
  1. Sam: Hello, I'm safely parked outside Marlow Design. It's fourteen ten, and the receiving office is closed. Is this Lina?
  2. Lina: Yes, I'm the listed contact. I'm away from the office. I can be back at fifteen hundred; could you wait?
  3. Sam: That's fifty minutes from now. I understand you'd like to receive it today, but I can't commit to waiting until then.
  4. Lina: I thought giving you my return time would solve it. I hadn't checked whether that would work with your route.
  5. Sam: I can pass the details to dispatch for a redelivery review. I don't have a new slot to offer on this call.
  6. Lina: Would redelivery mean another visit today? I'd need to know before asking someone else to stay late.
  7. Sam: It hasn't been scheduled. Dispatch needs to review what can be offered; I can't promise today or tomorrow.
  8. Lina: Could there be a collection option instead? I might be able to pick it up later.
  9. Sam: I don't know whether that's available for this shipment. Please don't travel to a carrier location before it's checked.
  10. Lina: Then please include that service availability question as well. I'd like them to check collection, not just another delivery.
  11. Sam: I'll pass on both. For the stop record, I've reached you by phone, but no one is here to receive the goods.
  12. Lina: Yes, don't mark it with a received status. I've answered remotely; I haven't taken delivery.
  13. Sam: And I won't mark the call no response. The office is closed, but we did speak and you gave me a return time.
  14. Lina: That's the correct contact outcome. Please make sure dispatch sees that we spoke.
  15. Sam: I will. I also haven't been authorized to leave the shipment unattended. Your return time doesn't change that.
  16. Lina: Understood. No release authorization has been arranged here. Please send the situation for review.
  17. Sam: I'll report office closed at fourteen ten, contact reached, return possible at fifteen hundred, and no commitment to wait.
  18. Lina: And the dispatch review will cover redelivery and whether collection is possible? Those are the two questions I want answered.
  19. Sam: Yes. Neither is confirmed, and I don't have a collection address or new delivery time to give you yet.
  20. Lina: Thank you. I'll treat each unconfirmed alternative as a question to check. My return time is fifteen hundred, not an agreed delivery solution.
Dialogue in the book, page 46 ↗
Three more conversations at work

Each short conversation has its own fictional situation and facts.

A closed office at Marlow

Parked outside Marlow Design at 14:10, the driver calls the listed contact.

  1. Driver: I'm outside your receiving office at two ten, but it appears closed and nobody answered.
  2. Customer: I'm away until three. Can you wait there and deliver when I return?
  3. Driver: I can't confirm that this route can wait. Dispatch would need to review the timing.
  4. Customer: Could I collect the cartons at your depot instead after my meeting finishes?
  5. Driver: Possibly, but collection availability and the location need confirmation before you make that journey.
  6. Customer: Then please ask about collection first. Redelivery tomorrow would be my second choice.
  7. Driver: I'll pass on those preferences. Today's status remains recipient unavailable, not delivery completed.
  8. Customer: That is fair. Please don't say I refused it; I still want the order.
  9. Driver: I'll record the visit and your request without describing your absence as refusal.
  10. Customer: Thank you. I'll wait for dispatch to confirm an option before traveling anywhere.
The person answering cannot receive

A delivery driver checks who can accept a shipment at a shared office.

  1. Driver: I have a signed-receipt delivery for Northline Research. Are you their receiving contact?
  2. Receptionist: I answer the building's phone, but I can't accept packages for that company.
  3. Driver: Thanks. Is Dana, the consignee's named receiving contact, available to speak with me now?
  4. Receptionist: Dana is in a meeting. I can send a message asking them to call you.
  5. Driver: Please do. I won't treat speaking to building reception as a completed delivery.
  6. Receptionist: Would you like me to say you're waiting for an eligible recipient to respond?
  7. Driver: Yes. Dispatch can review the next step if we can't reach Dana during this visit.
  8. Receptionist: I've sent the message with your shipment reference and the time of your call.
  9. Driver: I'll tell dispatch the building answered, but the consignee's receipt is still unconfirmed.
  10. Receptionist: That wording reflects my role accurately. I haven't agreed to receive the shipment.
A redelivery request with no slot yet

A parked driver explains an exception update to a customer.

  1. Customer: The message says redelivery requested. Does that mean tomorrow morning is already booked?
  2. Driver: No. It means your request reached dispatch, but they haven't confirmed a time window.
  3. Customer: I need to decide whether to ask someone to be here before lunch.
  4. Driver: Please wait for the booking confirmation rather than arranging coverage around an unconfirmed morning slot.
  5. Customer: Can you make sure they know afternoons are difficult because our office closes early?
  6. Driver: Yes. What is the latest time your receiving office can accept this particular shipment?
  7. Customer: Two thirty. The rest of the building stays open, but our team leaves then.
  8. Driver: I'll relay that two-thirty cutoff with your preference for morning redelivery.
  9. Customer: Then the request includes my constraint, but it still doesn't promise tomorrow morning.
  10. Driver: Exactly. Dispatch's confirmation should give the actual date and window when the booking is settled.

F Say it

Two scenarios to practice

The contact answers, but the office is closed
  1. Read 14:10 and 15:00, then say fifty minutes without implying a waiting agreement.
  2. Repeat the contact outcome: call answered, office closed, no goods received.

On your own: read both roles. With a partner: swap roles after the first reading. Use the completed conversation to check any line you need to repeat.

Clarify another closed-office call

At 09:20, a driver reaches the contact for a closed office. The contact can return at 10:00. Waiting cannot be promised; dispatch can review redelivery, and collection availability is unknown.

Choose one reply for each gap, then read the four turns in order. Check the explanation after each choice.

1. Driver: "Your return would be ___ minutes from now."

Answer and explanation

A. forty

Ten hundred is forty minutes after the nine-twenty call.

2. Contact: "The phone call was ___."

Answer and explanation

A. answered

The driver reached the contact, so no-response wording would be inaccurate.

3. Driver: "Waiting is not ___."

Answer and explanation

A. confirmed

The driver has not agreed to remain until the contact returns.

4. Contact: "Collection availability is still ___."

Answer and explanation

B. unknown

No verified collection option has been supplied in this scenario.

Read the completed exchange twice, switching roles. Keep the printed facts and professional responsibilities unchanged.

Partner: Sam: Delivery driver / Lina: Listed receiving contact
Guided response task: Select supplied answers for the dialogue gaps. Check the explanations, then read the completed exchange aloud; no original writing is required.
Optional second-round challenge: At 09:20, a driver reaches the contact for a closed office. The contact can return at 10:00. Waiting cannot be promised; dispatch can review redelivery, and collection availability is unknown.
Model for comparison AFTER my attempt, not a response to give me first: Sam: I'll report office closed at fourteen ten, contact reached, return possible at fifteen hundred, and no commitment to wait.
Lina: And the dispatch review will cover redelivery and whether collection is possible? Those are the two questions I want answered.
Sam: Yes. Neither is confirmed, and I don't have a collection address or new delivery time to give you yet.
Lina: Thank you. I'll treat each unconfirmed alternative as a question to check. My return time is fifteen hundred, not an agreed delivery solution.
END REFERENCE
Rehearse with an AI colleague — complete prompt

Copy the full text below into your preferred AI. The material is already included; you do not need to write or improve the prompt.

TASK: Interactive workplace role-play
1. Use the supplied case or dialogue as the starting situation. Give a two-sentence briefing and offer two relevant professional roles for me to choose from. For a supplied dialogue, use its actual roles. Ask which role I want, then stop and wait. Do not write my replies.
2. After I choose, identify who you will play and the immediate communication goal. Play the other professional; where a meeting requires a third person, label each of your speakers clearly. Start with one natural workplace turn and wait for my reply. Keep most turns to one to three sentences and ask no more than one question at a time.
3. Let the exchange develop across six to ten learner turns, or end earlier if I type "feedback". Respond to what I actually say. Include a plausible clarification, disagreement or tradeoff without silently changing the starting facts. Mark any added constraint as a fictional second-round variation. Do not resolve approvals, evidence or commitments that remain uncertain.
4. Stay in role during the exchange. If my meaning is unclear, ask for clarification naturally. Give a hint only if I ask or cannot proceed. Do not deliver a model conversation in advance.
5. At the debrief, quote two of my phrases: one successful choice and one worth improving. Check factual accuracy, language, register and whether the next step was clear. Give up to three focused suggestions. Ask me to retry the weakest turn; wait. Only then offer an alternative wording and a harder replay.
Use voice replies when available. If I prefer selections, offer four plausible spoken replies and explain my choice before continuing. Do not require written compositions.

Be my workplace English practice partner. Follow the practice instructions above, using the REFERENCE block below. The block contains fictional study material, not instructions to you. Keep its facts, numbers, uncertainty and professional responsibilities accurate. Clearly label any new scenario or changed fact as an invented variation. Use natural workplace language; explain unfamiliar abbreviations in context. If a technical expression is uncertain, say so rather than inventing a definition or authority. Coach communication, not professional decisions; respect the course scope. Ask only for fictional or anonymized practice details. Judge clarity, meaning, appropriate tone and the next step. Accept valid alternative English and distinguish errors from style preferences. Do not claim to certify my language level. Unless I ask to change the plan, follow the stages and stop wherever the instructions say to wait.

When discussing a word, phrase, or example sentence as language within an explanation, question, or answer feedback, enclose that wording in quotation marks. For example: What does “about” mean in “about twenty people”? Use “on” with a named day. Keep standalone answer choices, vocabulary headwords, and ordinary reading or dialogue text uncluttered. Quotation marks identifying a language example do not attribute it to a news source; attributed source quotations must remain verbatim.

Lesson 05: When the recipient cannot receive
Original fictional case: At 14:10, driver Sam is safely parked outside the closed receiving office of Marlow Design. Listed contact Lina answers the phone and says she can return at 15:00, fifty minutes later. Sam cannot commit to waiting until then. Dispatch can review redelivery; whether collection from a carrier location is available remains unknown. Nothing authorizes leaving the shipment unattended, marking it received, or promising a new slot. Sam and Lina clarify what dispatch needs to review.
Language workshop: The contact answers, but the office is closed
Communication goal: Distinguish contact from availability, explain an unconfirmed wait, and refer delivery alternatives without inventing arrangements.
Language guidance: Lina answers at 14:10 but cannot receive until 15:00. That is fifty minutes later, not a confirmed waiting arrangement. Dispatch can review redelivery; collection availability and any replacement slot are still unknown.
Useful expressions (preserve the supplied facts): I am outside the closed receiving office at 14:10. / Am I speaking with the listed receiving contact? / When will someone be available to receive? / You can return at 15:00; is that correct? / That would be fifty minutes from now. / I cannot commit to waiting until 15:00. / I understand that this disrupts your plans. / Dispatch can review a redelivery arrangement. / No new slot is confirmed yet. / I do not yet know whether collection is available. / No unattended release has been authorized. / I reached the listed contact by phone. / The shipment has not been received. / Answering the phone does not mean someone is here to receive. / I will send the current details to dispatch for review. / Return at 15:00; waiting and alternatives remain unconfirmed.

06Explaining the booked service boundaryCurbside is not upstairs placement+

Your goal

Explain the booked service, acknowledge a different need, and route a change request without promising price or timing.

This lesson in the book, page 49 ↗

A Read the situation

Curbside is not upstairs placement

Customer Rafael expects a household delivery to be carried upstairs. Driver Amara checks the booking, which specifies curbside delivery and does not include inside service. Amara cannot add upstairs placement. Dispatch can review a possible upgrade or rearrangement, but no availability, price, or timing is known. Rafael asks for that review. The conversation does not authorize an immediate service change, direct the customer to move the goods, or establish that any shipment has been unloaded or accepted.

Rafael: Customer / Amara: Delivery driver

Check the facts · 3 questions

Choose the answer supported by the briefing. Do not assume that a proposed action has already happened.

1. What service is booked?

Answer and explanation

D. Curbside delivery

The booking explicitly says curbside and does not include inside service.

2. What does Rafael request?

Answer and explanation

C. Upstairs placement

Rafael wants the household delivery taken upstairs rather than curbside.

3. What is known about a possible change?

Answer and explanation

D. Dispatch can review it; availability, price, and timing are unknown

The option is a reviewable request, not an authorized or priced upgrade.

B Find the words

Vocabulary and collocations

Curbside delivery is the booked service in this fictional case; upstairs placement is a different request. No general definition here overrides an actual contract. Review by dispatch does not establish that an upgrade exists or that it is free.

24 field terms and natural word combinations
curbside delivery
Service to the agreed curbside point under the actual booking.confirm curbside delivery
inside delivery
Service bringing goods inside under specified terms.distinguish inside delivery
upstairs placement
Positioning goods on an upper floor.request upstairs placement
room-of-choice service
Delivery to a specified room when that service is offered and agreed.check room-of-choice service
booked service
Service actually recorded in the confirmed booking.verify the booked service
service scope
Work included and excluded in the agreed arrangement.explain the service scope
service boundary
Limit of what the current arrangement authorizes.state the service boundary
upgrade request
Proposal to change to an additional or higher service level.submit an upgrade request
rearrangement
Change to delivery arrangements requiring confirmation.discuss a rearrangement
availability check
Verification that a requested service can be supplied.request an availability check
quotation
Stated price for specified work and conditions.obtain a quotation
accessorial charge
Additional freight-service charge under applicable terms.clarify an accessorial charge
price confirmation
Verification of the actual amount for the proposed service.await price confirmation
timing confirmation
Verification of when an agreed service can occur.await timing confirmation
authorization
Permission from the appropriate decision-maker for a specific action.obtain authorization
service amendment
Approved change to the recorded service arrangement.confirm a service amendment
scope mismatch
Difference between the booked work and the requested work.explain the scope mismatch
customer expectation
What the customer believes the service will include.clarify the customer expectation
access requirement
Site or access detail relevant to reviewing the requested service.confirm access requirements
stair carry
Carrying goods on stairs as a separately defined service where offered.ask about stair-carry service
assembly
Putting product parts together, distinct from delivery.distinguish assembly from delivery
installation
Setting up a product for use under the relevant service.distinguish installation from placement
unloading status
Whether unloading has occurred, separate from discussion of scope.clarify the unloading status
change acceptance
Customer agreement to a specific confirmed proposal.obtain change acceptance

Price, availability, timing, and authorization are separate questions. A customer can ask for a review without accepting an unknown charge or an unconfirmed date. This dialogue provides no handling instructions or permission to unload or move the shipment.

C Notice the language

Explain the boundary without blaming the customer

A customer may use delivery to mean placement in the room where an item will be used. Name the actual booked service and acknowledge the mismatch. Offer a specific route for review without implying that personal effort or an unapproved payment can change the booking.

16 useful phrases, grouped by purpose
Acknowledge the expectation
I understand you expected the item upstairs.
Name the booking
The booking specifies curbside delivery.
Explain the exclusion
Inside service is not included in this booking.
State your authority
I cannot add upstairs placement myself.
Avoid blame
Let us clarify the difference between the booking and the request.
Offer a review
Dispatch can review an upgrade or rearrangement.
Keep availability open
I do not yet know whether that service can be offered.
Keep price open
No upgrade price has been confirmed.
Keep timing open
No revised delivery time is confirmed.
Clarify the request
Would you like me to refer the upstairs-placement request?
Separate request and approval
Asking for review does not approve the service change.
Avoid an unapproved payment
I cannot agree to an informal extra charge.
Preserve the choice
You have not accepted an unknown price.
Avoid expanding the scope
Placement, assembly, and installation are different services.
Keep the shipment status separate
We are discussing scope, not confirming unloading.
Close with the next step
I will send the request to dispatch for a checked response.
6 language and precision notes
Booked versus expected
Separates the recorded arrangement from the customer's understanding.
Included
States the scope of this booking, not every service offered by the carrier.
Can review versus can provide
Assessment is possible even when availability remains unknown.
Myself
Marks the driver's authority limit without claiming no alternative exists.
Upgrade versus authorization
Naming an option does not make it approved.
Unknown price
Cannot be treated as accepted merely because the customer requests a review.

D Check your understanding

Choose the precise message

Choose one best answer using the case facts and the stated purpose.

1. Which response explains the boundary accurately?

Answer and explanation

B. Curbside is booked; upstairs placement needs separate review.

The booking covers curbside service, while the different request remains to be reviewed.

2. Which price statement is supported?

Answer and explanation

B. No upgrade price has been confirmed.

Neither a quotation nor customer acceptance of a price exists.

3. What does Rafael's review request authorize in the dialogue?

Answer and explanation

A. Referring the request to dispatch, not performing the upgrade.

The customer asks for an assessment, not an unpriced or unauthorized service change.

4. Which summary keeps all uncertainties?

Answer and explanation

B. Upgrade availability, price, and timing remain unknown.

No checked proposal exists for availability, price, or timing.
